---
**Overview**

Sheikh Hasina was Bangladesh’s prime minister in 2023 and remained the dominant political actor through the 7 January 2024 election campaign. Under the constitution, executive power sits with the prime minister and cabinet, while the president is formally head of state and generally acts on the prime minister’s advice. In practice, Hasina’s authority was broad, but not unlimited: Parliament’s formal oversight existed, yet opposition weakness, judicial dependence, and external pressure from the United States and other partners constrained the government unevenly.

What follows is an orientation memo, not a final judgment. I have kept one underlying fact per record and separated retrospective material where needed.

**Evidence Records**

1. **Constitutional executive power sat with the PM, not the president**
- Fact: Bangladesh’s constitutional design gave the prime minister and cabinet the operative executive authority in 2023. The president was head of state and generally acted on the prime minister’s advice, so Hasina’s office carried real governing power rather than a merely ceremonial role.
- Why it matters: This is the baseline for attributing decisions on security, elections, economic policy, and repression.
- Strongest source: The Constitution of Bangladesh, official text, [bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-367/act-chapter-print-680.html) and [bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-print-367.html).
- Locator: Article 55 and 56 for the cabinet and prime minister; Article 48 for the president’s role.
- Period fit: Inherited constitutional structure, active in 2023.
- Connection to ruler: Direct and strong.
- Complicating evidence: None on the formal allocation of authority; the complication is practical, not textual.
- Cautions: Constitutional text does not show how power actually functioned in practice.
- Supporting sources: Banglapedia’s constitutional summary, Constitution Project country profile.

2. **Hasina governed through a dominant-party system with weak parliamentary checks**
- Fact: By 2023 the Awami League had consolidated political power, and the National Parliament had reduced capacity to check the executive because opposition representation was thin and the system was heavily tilted toward the ruling party.
- Why it matters: This explains why formal checks existed but were not effective constraints.
- Strongest source: Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2024: Bangladesh*, [freedomhouse.org](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024).
- Locator: Overview and C2 discussion on policy set by the ruling AL and weak checks.
- Period fit: Retrospective assessment of 2023 conditions.
- Connection to ruler: Strong, though the source is evaluative rather than documentary.
- Complicating evidence: The constitution still required cabinet responsibility to Parliament, and local elections sometimes produced opposition or independent wins.
- Cautions: Freedom House is a high-quality secondary assessment, not a primary record.
- Supporting sources: Reuters reporting on the opposition boycott; the Bangladeshi Constitution; U.S. State Department 2023 report.

3. **The opposition was harassed, and the 2024 election was prepared in a coercive environment**
- Fact: In the months before the January 2024 election, the BNP and allied opposition faced arrests, cases, intimidation, and a boycott decision; this was the political environment of late 2023 under Hasina.
- Why it matters: It goes to legitimacy, competition, and the ruler’s effective room to govern without genuine electoral contest.
- Strongest source: Reuters, “Bangladesh’s main opposition to boycott vote if Hasina stays put,” [ThePrint/Reuters feed](https://theprint.in/world/bangladeshs-main-opposition-to-boycott-vote-if-hasina-stays-put/1827169/), 1 Nov. 2023.
- Locator: Lead paragraph on boycott threat, crackdown, jailed or exiled leadership, and U.S. visa policy.
- Period fit: Directly about 2023.
- Connection to ruler: Strong, because the government controlled policing and election conditions.
- Complicating evidence: The opposition’s boycott was also a strategic choice, not just a response to repression.
- Cautions: Reuters is reporting contemporaneous claims, not adjudicating them.
- Supporting sources: Human Rights Watch 2023/2024 country reporting; Freedom House 2024; U.S. State Department 2023 report.

4. **Repression and media control intensified, including use of cyber laws**
- Fact: In 2023 authorities continued arresting critics under the Digital Security Act, and in September 2023 Bangladesh replaced it with the Cyber Security Act, which retained many of the same repressive features and broad speech restrictions.
- Why it matters: This is central to political freedom, media freedom, and the reliability of public information.
- Strongest source: Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Bangladesh*, [hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh) and the official law text, [bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-print-1457/section-print-52866.html).
- Locator: HRW section on arrests of journalists and critics; Cyber Security Act section 42 on warrantless search, seizure, and arrest.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Connection to ruler: Strong. The law was enacted by the government; enforcement was state action.
- Complicating evidence: The government said the new law was a reform of the old one, but rights groups argued the core structure remained abusive.
- Cautions: HRW is a secondary human-rights source; the statute text shows powers but not actual usage levels.
- Supporting sources: U.S. State Department 2023 report; Freedom House internet-freedom report; Reuters Institute reporting on press constraints.

5. **Information conditions were poor and likely worse for opposition, victims, and journalists**
- Fact: Reliable information on abuses was hard to obtain because critics faced arrests, surveillance, intimidation, and legal harassment; media and civil society operated under pressure, and social media/online reporting were manipulated.
- Why it matters: This affects every later evidence judgment, especially on violence, corruption, and election conduct.
- Strongest source: Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2024: Bangladesh*, [freedomhouse.org](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-net/2024).
- Locator: Key Developments for June 1, 2023 to May 31, 2024.
- Period fit: Covers late 2023 and the January 2024 election period.
- Connection to ruler: Strong, because the ruling party and state apparatus shaped the environment.
- Complicating evidence: Some local outlets and international observers still reported aggressively, so the information environment was degraded rather than sealed.
- Cautions: Freedom House is a synthesized assessment and may lag specific events.
- Supporting sources: U.S. State Department 2023 report; HRW country chapter; Reuters Institute note on Bangladesh journalism conditions.

6. **The economy grew, but inflation and external pressure were serious**
- Fact: Bangladesh’s economy in 2023 was not in collapse, but it faced high inflation, import compression, and balance-of-payments stress; IMF said real GDP growth slowed to about 6 percent in FY23 while headline inflation hit a decade high of 9.9 percent in August 2023.
- Why it matters: This is the best compact summary of Hasina’s economic record in the period: continued growth, but mounting cost-of-living pressure and macroeconomic strain.
- Strongest source: IMF, *Bangladesh: 2023 Article IV Consultation and First Review*, [imf.org](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/12/12/pr23440-bangladesh-imf-exec-board-concludes-2023-art-iv-consult-completes-1st-rev-ecf-eef-rsf).
- Locator: Lines discussing FY23 growth, August 2023 inflation, and policy recommendations.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Connection to ruler: Moderate to strong. The government influenced policy, but global shocks mattered materially.
- Complicating evidence: IMF also noted resilience in exports and ongoing reform efforts.
- Cautions: IMF data is macro-level and does not by itself prove welfare outcomes for households.
- Supporting sources: IMF Selected Issues paper; Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics publications if needed in the next pass.

**Later Retrospective Evidence**
- Freedom House 2024, HRW World Report 2024, and the ACLED pieces are retrospective on the 2023 environment and early 2024 election dynamics. They are useful for orientation, but they should be treated as after-the-fact synthesis rather than contemporaneous primary evidence.

**Information Environment**
- Reliable information was available, but unevenly.
- The main distortions were repression, intimidation, politicized policing, and legal risk for critics.
- Election-related facts are especially sensitive because the opposition was weakened, turnout was contested, and the state controlled much of the reporting environment.
- Economic facts are more reliable than rights or accountability claims, but even there, household experience can be obscured by aggregate indicators.
- For violence, repression, and corruption, the next researcher should look for triangulation across official text, court records, independent monitoring, and local reporting.

**Questions For Deeper Research**
1. How directly did Hasina personally direct opposition prosecutions, or were these largely delegated to party and security actors?
2. Which institutions, if any, checked the executive in practice during 2023: courts, election bodies, civil service, or military leadership?
3. How much of the 2023 cost-of-living crisis was policy-driven versus imported through global energy, food, and FX shocks?
4. What was the scale of political violence in 2023 by month, and how much was attributable to state forces versus party activists?
5. Which specific journalists, outlets, or NGOs were most constrained, and what were the strongest documented cases?
6. What concrete policy achievements, if any, best support Hasina’s own program claims in infrastructure, social welfare, and energy?

**Corroborating Sources**
- [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2024: Bangladesh](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024)
- [Freedom House, Freedom on the Net 2024: Bangladesh](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-net/2024)
- [U.S. State Department 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Bangladesh via ecoi.net](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- [Human Rights Watch, Bangladesh: Violence, Repression Ahead of General Elections](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/01/12/bangladesh-violence-repression-ahead-general-elections)
- [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2024: Bangladesh](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- [IMF Bangladesh Article IV / First Review, Dec. 2023](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/12/12/pr23440-bangladesh-imf-exec-board-concludes-2023-art-iv-consult-completes-1st-rev-ecf-eef-rsf)
- [Bangladesh Constitution, official text](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-367/act-chapter-print-680.html)
- [Cyber Security Act, 2023, official text](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-print-1457/section-print-52866.html)
- [Reuters report on the opposition boycott threat, Nov. 2023](https://theprint.in/world/bangladeshs-main-opposition-to-boycott-vote-if-hasina-stays-put/1827169/)

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**
- Official Bangladesh Election Commission material on the January 2024 election results and turnout.
- Primary reporting or court records on the most important 2023 political prosecution cases.
- UN OHCHR material on Bangladesh’s human rights environment in 2023.
- ACLED’s Bangladesh election analysis for event-level violence counts.
- Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics releases for cost of living, employment, and household welfare in 2023.
- A high-quality source on the Rohingya situation and border/security pressure in 2023.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**

Sheikh Hasina’s 2023 catastrophic-risk record in Bangladesh is mostly about nuclear-energy governance, not nuclear weapons. Bangladesh was not a nuclear-weapon state; it remained bound by the NPT, the TPNW, and IAEA safeguards, while moving ahead with the first Rooppur nuclear power plant. The state also maintained a dedicated safeguards/security bureaucracy and, in parallel, treated cyber threats as a serious national-security issue. I did not find evidence of nuclear brinkmanship, proliferation behavior, or use of nuclear capability to shield aggression. The main unresolved question is not weapons policy, but whether the government’s nuclear-safety, security, and dual-use-risk institutions were robust enough in practice as the program scaled up.

Information quality is mixed. Formal treaty status and reactor/custody facts are relatively solid. Claims about safety culture, operational rigor, and crisis handling rely heavily on government or quasi-government sources and need more independent corroboration.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Bangladesh had no nuclear weapons program and remained under safeguards**
- Claim: Bangladesh was a non-nuclear-weapon state with NPT safeguards, an Additional Protocol, and no declared nuclear weapons or explosive devices under its control.
- Why it matters: This sets the baseline for all 1B lenses. Hasina was not managing an arsenal; the relevant issue is civilian nuclear governance and nonproliferation compliance.
- Strong sources:
  - IAEA Country Nuclear Power Profiles, Bangladesh: [IAEA CNPP](https://cnpp.iaea.org/public/countries/BD/profile/highlights)
  - IAEA/UN treaty profile: [UNTC TPNW record](https://treaties.un.org/Pages/showDetails.aspx?objid=08000002804c2398)
  - UNODA declarations page showing Bangladesh’s TPNW declaration deposit: [UNODA](https://disarmament.unoda.org/en/our-work/weapons-mass-destruction/nuclear-weapons/treaty-prohibition-nuclear-weapons/declarations-pursuant-article-2-tpnw)
  - BAEC safeguards/security page: [BAEC Nuclear Safeguards and Security Division](https://baec.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6922dd33933eb65569e13ea5)
- Locator:
  - IAEA CNPP: “Bangladesh is a newcomer to nuclear power, with two power reactors under construction.”
  - UNTC: Bangladesh signed the TPNW on 20 Sept. 2017 and ratified on 26 Sept. 2019.
  - BAEC: safeguards ensure nuclear material is used only for peaceful purposes; division exists to plan, implement, and evaluate security activities.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong and direct for the nonproliferation baseline.
- Cautions: IAEA and BAEC summarize legal/organizational status; they do not prove day-to-day effectiveness.

2. **Rooppur advanced during 2023 under a civilian-energy framing**
- Claim: Bangladesh’s first nuclear power plant at Rooppur was under construction in 2023, and uranium fuel shipments began arriving in October 2023.
- Why it matters: This is the core 2023 nuclear policy action. It shows Hasina prioritizing nuclear electricity as a development and energy-security project, not weapons capability.
- Strong sources:
  - Reuters via Fiji Times: [Bangladesh gets first uranium shipment from Russia for nuclear power plant](https://www.fijitimes.com.fj/bangladesh-gets-first-uranium-shipment-from-russia-for-nuclear-power-plant/)
  - Dhaka Tribune reporting on later fuel shipments: [Fifth batch of uranium reaches Rooppur plant](https://www.dhakatribune.com/bangladesh/329181/fifth-batch-of-uranium-reaches-rooppur-plant)
  - IAEA PRIS country data: [Bangladesh country details](https://pris.iaea.org/PRIS/CountryStatistics/CountryDetails.aspx?current=BD)
- Locator:
  - Reuters story: 5 Oct. 2023 reporting the first Russian uranium shipment and the plant’s $12.65 billion cost.
  - PRIS: both Rooppur units listed as under construction.
- Period fit: Directly target-period.
- Attribution: Strong for project status; moderate for policy significance.
- Cautions: The Reuters report is about one ceremony and fuel shipment, not long-run safety performance.

3. **Hasina publicly framed nuclear power as peaceful and tied to energy security**
- Claim: In 2023 Hasina’s public statements presented Rooppur and nuclear power as peaceful, developmental, and energy-security oriented.
- Why it matters: This is the clearest evidence for 1B.2, and partly for 1B.1 and 1B.5.
- Strong sources:
  - BSS report on Rooppur fuel delivery and PM remarks: [Rooppur power plant is another step towards Smart Bangladesh: PM](https://www.bssnews.net/news-flash/150982)
  - BSS report on energy strategy: [Discovery of new gas fields helps establish Smart Bangladesh: PM](https://www.bssnews.net/news/140839)
- Locator:
  - BSS fuel-delivery story: the quoted line that Bangladesh would “use nuclear power to protect peace” and that the Russian Federation would take back spent fuel.
  - BSS energy message: Hasina linked energy security to rational resource use, new gas fields, and alternative energy.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate. This is official/near-official rhetoric, not independent validation.
- Cautions: Party-state media can overstate or sanitize policy; use only as rhetoric unless corroborated.

4. **Bangladesh maintained formal nuclear-safety and safeguards institutions**
- Claim: Bangladesh had an established nuclear-safeguards/security bureaucracy responsible for IAEA reporting, physical protection, import/export controls, and records of nuclear material.
- Why it matters: This is the institutional backbone for 1B.3, 1B.6, and part of 1B.7.
- Strong source: [BAEC Nuclear Safeguards and Security Division](https://baec.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6922dd33933eb65569e13ea5)
- Locator:
  - The page states the division was created in 2008.
  - It says the division handles safeguards reports to the IAEA, physical protection, SSAC maintenance, and international legal instruments needed for RNPP.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for institutional existence; weaker for actual effectiveness.
- Cautions: Institutional description comes from the responsible agency itself.

5. **Rooppur had a defined physical-protection model and monitored security arrangement**
- Claim: The Rooppur project said it had adopted IAEA-guided physical-protection measures, a design-basis-threat analysis, and a monitoring team led by the Rajshahi divisional commissioner.
- Why it matters: This is the best direct source on claimed safety/security controls around the plant.
- Strong source: [Rooppur nuclear plant physical protection page](https://rooppurnpp.gov.bd/site/page/d08b5e3b-165e-45df-9e83-5c35ba4d4f11/Physical-Protection-System-)
- Locator:
  - Bengali text describing security as a critical issue.
  - Mention of a concept design for PPS using IAEA guidance and BAERA legal requirements.
  - Mention of a monitoring team for overall security and project implementation.
- Period fit: Inherited program context active in 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong for claimed controls; weak on independent verification.
- Cautions: Self-description by the project owner; no independent audit here.

6. **Bangladesh’s civilian nuclear program sat inside a broader nonproliferation commitment**
- Claim: Bangladesh had longstanding treaty commitments to IAEA safeguards, the NPT, the CTBT, and later the TPNW, and in 2023 continued to present itself as committed to peaceful nuclear use and disarmament.
- Why it matters: This is the key evidence for 1B.4 and part of 1B.6.
- Strong sources:
  - IAEA 2013 country profile treaty table: [Bangladesh IAEA country profile](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/CNPP2013_CD/countryprofiles/Bangladesh/Bangladesh.htm)
  - UNTC TPNW record: [UNTC](https://treaties.un.org/Pages/showDetails.aspx?objid=08000002804c2398)
  - ICAN country page summarizing the TPNW status and safeguards: [ICAN Bangladesh](https://www.icanw.org/bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - IAEA profile lists NPT, safeguards agreement, Additional Protocol, CTBT.
  - UNTC lists Bangladesh signature and ratification dates for TPNW.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for treaty status.
- Cautions: The 2013 IAEA profile is older, so use as baseline, not as 2023 operational proof.

7. **Bangladesh treated cyber as a serious national-security threat in 2023**
- Claim: Government cyber-response material in 2023 described intensifying threats, including phishing, DDoS attacks, data leaks, and infostealer malware targeting government and law-enforcement systems.
- Why it matters: For 1B.9, cyber is the clearest catastrophic dual-use area with visible state attention in 2023.
- Strong sources:
  - [Bangladesh Cyber Threat Landscape 2023](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/documents/cyber-threat-landscape-2023)
  - [Press Release: Situational Alert on Cyber Threats, June 2023](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/news/press-release-on-cyber-threats-june-2023)
  - [Press Release July 08 2023: Alert on Cyber Threats](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/news/press-release-july-08-2023-cyber-threats)
  - [Infostealer alert](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/alerts/cyberthreatalert-infostealer)
- Locator:
  - The 2023 landscape page says Bangladesh faced an intensifying cyber threat environment, with attacks on government and law-enforcement entities.
  - The June and July 2023 alerts and later infostealer warning show sustained operational concern.
- Period fit: Directly 2023, though the landscape summary was published later.
- Attribution: Moderate. These are official cybersecurity materials, useful for threat environment and state posture.
- Cautions: The 2024 summary is retrospective; government cyber publications can understate breaches or overstate readiness.

8. **Bangladesh’s broader existential-risk posture was not safer in a strong sense, but it remained non-nuclear-weapon and treaty-bound**
- Claim: By the end of 2023 Bangladesh had advanced civilian nuclear infrastructure without any documented move toward weapons, and it remained inside the nonproliferation regime.
- Why it matters: This is the best available answer for 1B.10.
- Strong sources: Combined inference from the IAEA CNPP, UNTC TPNW record, BAEC safeguards page, and the Rooppur/Reuters materials above.
- Locator: As above.
- Period fit: End-of-2023 posture.
- Attribution: Inference from multiple sources, not a single explicit statement.
- Cautions: This does not establish that the posture was operationally safe, only that it remained non-weaponized and formally constrained.

**Lens Disposition**

- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk:** Partial evidence. Hasina’s government supported civilian nuclear power and nonproliferation, but I found no distinct 2023 policy package aimed at reducing catastrophic conflict risk beyond normal treaty compliance.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture:** Partial evidence. Bangladesh ended 2023 still non-nuclear-weapon and treaty-bound, but the nuclear-energy program expanded and independent safety assessment is thin.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Supported. The PM publicly framed nuclear power as peaceful and linked it to energy security.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control:** Partial evidence. Formal safeguards and physical-protection structures are documented; actual effectiveness remains unproven here.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections:** Supported on baseline treaty status and continuing adherence.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression:** No evidence found of such use.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation:** Supported at the formal level; no contrary evidence found in the inspected sources.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise:** Partial evidence only. BAEC and Rooppur cite expert groups and IAEA-guided design, but independence from the executive is unclear.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation:** No direct crisis case found in the inspected 2023 record.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks:** Supported for cyber threat recognition and response posture; weak on AI, biotech, and other dual-use domains.

- More direct 2023/2024 Reuters or AP reporting on Rooppur safety and Russian loan dependence
- UNODA and IAEA records on Bangladesh’s 2023 disarmament statements at UN forums
- Independent reporting on BAERA staffing, inspection capability, and political independence
- Any 2023 audit or parliamentary material on nuclear project spending, delays, or safety findings
- Country-specific cyber incident reporting from independent security researchers, not only government CIRT material

**Inspection notes**
I inspected these candidate families directly: IAEA, UNODA/UN treaty records, BAEC, Rooppur project site, Reuters-fed reporting, BSS, CIRT, OPWC, ICAN, PRIS, and one OHCHR communication summary. I also attempted the World Bank climate report and ADB annual report as contextual checks, but they were not materially useful for this chapter’s narrow nuclear/existential-risk question.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**

Sheikh Hasina’s 2023 catastrophic-risk record in Bangladesh is mostly about nuclear-energy governance, not nuclear weapons. Bangladesh was not a nuclear-weapon state; it remained bound by the NPT, the TPNW, and IAEA safeguards, while moving ahead with the first Rooppur nuclear power plant. The state also maintained a dedicated safeguards/security bureaucracy and, in parallel, treated cyber threats as a serious national-security issue. I did not find evidence of nuclear brinkmanship, proliferation behavior, or use of nuclear capability to shield aggression. The main unresolved question is not weapons policy, but whether the government’s nuclear-safety, security, and dual-use-risk institutions were robust enough in practice as the program scaled up.

Information quality is mixed. Formal treaty status and reactor/custody facts are relatively solid. Claims about safety culture, operational rigor, and crisis handling rely heavily on government or quasi-government sources and need more independent corroboration.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Bangladesh had no nuclear weapons program and remained under safeguards**
- Claim: Bangladesh was a non-nuclear-weapon state with NPT safeguards, an Additional Protocol, and no declared nuclear weapons or explosive devices under its control.
- Why it matters: This sets the baseline for all 1B lenses. Hasina was not managing an arsenal; the relevant issue is civilian nuclear governance and nonproliferation compliance.
- Strong sources:
  - IAEA Country Nuclear Power Profiles, Bangladesh: [IAEA CNPP](https://cnpp.iaea.org/public/countries/BD/profile/highlights)
  - IAEA/UN treaty profile: [UNTC TPNW record](https://treaties.un.org/Pages/showDetails.aspx?objid=08000002804c2398)
  - UNODA declarations page showing Bangladesh’s TPNW declaration deposit: [UNODA](https://disarmament.unoda.org/en/our-work/weapons-mass-destruction/nuclear-weapons/treaty-prohibition-nuclear-weapons/declarations-pursuant-article-2-tpnw)
  - BAEC safeguards/security page: [BAEC Nuclear Safeguards and Security Division](https://baec.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6922dd33933eb65569e13ea5)
- Locator:
  - IAEA CNPP: “Bangladesh is a newcomer to nuclear power, with two power reactors under construction.”
  - UNTC: Bangladesh signed the TPNW on 20 Sept. 2017 and ratified on 26 Sept. 2019.
  - BAEC: safeguards ensure nuclear material is used only for peaceful purposes; division exists to plan, implement, and evaluate security activities.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong and direct for the nonproliferation baseline.
- Cautions: IAEA and BAEC summarize legal/organizational status; they do not prove day-to-day effectiveness.

2. **Rooppur advanced during 2023 under a civilian-energy framing**
- Claim: Bangladesh’s first nuclear power plant at Rooppur was under construction in 2023, and uranium fuel shipments began arriving in October 2023.
- Why it matters: This is the core 2023 nuclear policy action. It shows Hasina prioritizing nuclear electricity as a development and energy-security project, not weapons capability.
- Strong sources:
  - Reuters via Fiji Times: [Bangladesh gets first uranium shipment from Russia for nuclear power plant](https://www.fijitimes.com.fj/bangladesh-gets-first-uranium-shipment-from-russia-for-nuclear-power-plant/)
  - Dhaka Tribune reporting on later fuel shipments: [Fifth batch of uranium reaches Rooppur plant](https://www.dhakatribune.com/bangladesh/329181/fifth-batch-of-uranium-reaches-rooppur-plant)
  - IAEA PRIS country data: [Bangladesh country details](https://pris.iaea.org/PRIS/CountryStatistics/CountryDetails.aspx?current=BD)
- Locator:
  - Reuters story: 5 Oct. 2023 reporting the first Russian uranium shipment and the plant’s $12.65 billion cost.
  - PRIS: both Rooppur units listed as under construction.
- Period fit: Directly target-period.
- Attribution: Strong for project status; moderate for policy significance.
- Cautions: The Reuters report is about one ceremony and fuel shipment, not long-run safety performance.

3. **Hasina publicly framed nuclear power as peaceful and tied to energy security**
- Claim: In 2023 Hasina’s public statements presented Rooppur and nuclear power as peaceful, developmental, and energy-security oriented.
- Why it matters: This is the clearest evidence for 1B.2, and partly for 1B.1 and 1B.5.
- Strong sources:
  - BSS report on Rooppur fuel delivery and PM remarks: [Rooppur power plant is another step towards Smart Bangladesh: PM](https://www.bssnews.net/news-flash/150982)
  - BSS report on energy strategy: [Discovery of new gas fields helps establish Smart Bangladesh: PM](https://www.bssnews.net/news/140839)
- Locator:
  - BSS fuel-delivery story: the quoted line that Bangladesh would “use nuclear power to protect peace” and that the Russian Federation would take back spent fuel.
  - BSS energy message: Hasina linked energy security to rational resource use, new gas fields, and alternative energy.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate. This is official/near-official rhetoric, not independent validation.
- Cautions: Party-state media can overstate or sanitize policy; use only as rhetoric unless corroborated.

4. **Bangladesh maintained formal nuclear-safety and safeguards institutions**
- Claim: Bangladesh had an established nuclear-safeguards/security bureaucracy responsible for IAEA reporting, physical protection, import/export controls, and records of nuclear material.
- Why it matters: This is the institutional backbone for 1B.3, 1B.6, and part of 1B.7.
- Strong source: [BAEC Nuclear Safeguards and Security Division](https://baec.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6922dd33933eb65569e13ea5)
- Locator:
  - The page states the division was created in 2008.
  - It says the division handles safeguards reports to the IAEA, physical protection, SSAC maintenance, and international legal instruments needed for RNPP.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for institutional existence; weaker for actual effectiveness.
- Cautions: Institutional description comes from the responsible agency itself.

5. **Rooppur had a defined physical-protection model and monitored security arrangement**
- Claim: The Rooppur project said it had adopted IAEA-guided physical-protection measures, a design-basis-threat analysis, and a monitoring team led by the Rajshahi divisional commissioner.
- Why it matters: This is the best direct source on claimed safety/security controls around the plant.
- Strong source: [Rooppur nuclear plant physical protection page](https://rooppurnpp.gov.bd/site/page/d08b5e3b-165e-45df-9e83-5c35ba4d4f11/Physical-Protection-System-)
- Locator:
  - Bengali text describing security as a critical issue.
  - Mention of a concept design for PPS using IAEA guidance and BAERA legal requirements.
  - Mention of a monitoring team for overall security and project implementation.
- Period fit: Inherited program context active in 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong for claimed controls; weak on independent verification.
- Cautions: Self-description by the project owner; no independent audit here.

6. **Bangladesh’s civilian nuclear program sat inside a broader nonproliferation commitment**
- Claim: Bangladesh had longstanding treaty commitments to IAEA safeguards, the NPT, the CTBT, and later the TPNW, and in 2023 continued to present itself as committed to peaceful nuclear use and disarmament.
- Why it matters: This is the key evidence for 1B.4 and part of 1B.6.
- Strong sources:
  - IAEA 2013 country profile treaty table: [Bangladesh IAEA country profile](https://www-pub.iaea.org/MTCD/Publications/PDF/CNPP2013_CD/countryprofiles/Bangladesh/Bangladesh.htm)
  - UNTC TPNW record: [UNTC](https://treaties.un.org/Pages/showDetails.aspx?objid=08000002804c2398)
  - ICAN country page summarizing the TPNW status and safeguards: [ICAN Bangladesh](https://www.icanw.org/bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - IAEA profile lists NPT, safeguards agreement, Additional Protocol, CTBT.
  - UNTC lists Bangladesh signature and ratification dates for TPNW.
- Period fit: Inherited baseline active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for treaty status.
- Cautions: The 2013 IAEA profile is older, so use as baseline, not as 2023 operational proof.

7. **Bangladesh treated cyber as a serious national-security threat in 2023**
- Claim: Government cyber-response material in 2023 described intensifying threats, including phishing, DDoS attacks, data leaks, and infostealer malware targeting government and law-enforcement systems.
- Why it matters: For 1B.9, cyber is the clearest catastrophic dual-use area with visible state attention in 2023.
- Strong sources:
  - [Bangladesh Cyber Threat Landscape 2023](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/documents/cyber-threat-landscape-2023)
  - [Press Release: Situational Alert on Cyber Threats, June 2023](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/news/press-release-on-cyber-threats-june-2023)
  - [Press Release July 08 2023: Alert on Cyber Threats](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/news/press-release-july-08-2023-cyber-threats)
  - [Infostealer alert](https://www.cirt.gov.bd/alerts/cyberthreatalert-infostealer)
- Locator:
  - The 2023 landscape page says Bangladesh faced an intensifying cyber threat environment, with attacks on government and law-enforcement entities.
  - The June and July 2023 alerts and later infostealer warning show sustained operational concern.
- Period fit: Directly 2023, though the landscape summary was published later.
- Attribution: Moderate. These are official cybersecurity materials, useful for threat environment and state posture.
- Cautions: The 2024 summary is retrospective; government cyber publications can understate breaches or overstate readiness.

8. **Bangladesh’s broader existential-risk posture was not safer in a strong sense, but it remained non-nuclear-weapon and treaty-bound**
- Claim: By the end of 2023 Bangladesh had advanced civilian nuclear infrastructure without any documented move toward weapons, and it remained inside the nonproliferation regime.
- Why it matters: This is the best available answer for 1B.10.
- Strong sources: Combined inference from the IAEA CNPP, UNTC TPNW record, BAEC safeguards page, and the Rooppur/Reuters materials above.
- Locator: As above.
- Period fit: End-of-2023 posture.
- Attribution: Inference from multiple sources, not a single explicit statement.
- Cautions: This does not establish that the posture was operationally safe, only that it remained non-weaponized and formally constrained.

**Lens Disposition**

- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk:** Partial evidence. Hasina’s government supported civilian nuclear power and nonproliferation, but I found no distinct 2023 policy package aimed at reducing catastrophic conflict risk beyond normal treaty compliance.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture:** Partial evidence. Bangladesh ended 2023 still non-nuclear-weapon and treaty-bound, but the nuclear-energy program expanded and independent safety assessment is thin.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Supported. The PM publicly framed nuclear power as peaceful and linked it to energy security.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control:** Partial evidence. Formal safeguards and physical-protection structures are documented; actual effectiveness remains unproven here.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections:** Supported on baseline treaty status and continuing adherence.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression:** No evidence found of such use.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation:** Supported at the formal level; no contrary evidence found in the inspected sources.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise:** Partial evidence only. BAEC and Rooppur cite expert groups and IAEA-guided design, but independence from the executive is unclear.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation:** No direct crisis case found in the inspected 2023 record.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks:** Supported for cyber threat recognition and response posture; weak on AI, biotech, and other dual-use domains.

**Promising leads still needing inspection**
- [IAEA country profile and/or PRIS reactor pages for Rooppur operating status details](https://pris.iaea.org/PRIS/CountryStatistics/CountryDetails.aspx?current=BD)
- Official Rooppur project fuel-handling and emergency-preparedness documents
- More direct 2023/2024 Reuters or AP reporting on Rooppur safety and Russian loan dependence
- UNODA and IAEA records on Bangladesh’s 2023 disarmament statements at UN forums
- Independent reporting on BAERA staffing, inspection capability, and political independence
- Any 2023 audit or parliamentary material on nuclear project spending, delays, or safety findings
- Country-specific cyber incident reporting from independent security researchers, not only government CIRT material

**Inspection notes**
I inspected these candidate families directly: IAEA, UNODA/UN treaty records, BAEC, Rooppur project site, Reuters-fed reporting, BSS, CIRT, OPWC, ICAN, PRIS, and one OHCHR communication summary. I also attempted the World Bank climate report and ADB annual report as contextual checks, but they were not materially useful for this chapter’s narrow nuclear/existential-risk question.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Orientation**

Bangladesh did not enter 2023 as an interstate belligerent, and Sheikh Hasina was not directing a conventional war effort. The relevant peace-and-security file was the Rohingya crisis with Myanmar: bilateral repatriation talks, UN-backed humanitarian response, and continued reliance on diplomacy rather than force. Bangladesh also remained a major UN peacekeeping contributor, which is the clearest 2023 evidence of lawful security posture. I found no evidence in the inspected record of Bangladesh initiating war, expanding a proxy conflict, or using military force outside defensive or border-security concerns.

The evidence base is uneven. Diplomatic and UN records are solid for what was said and formally attempted. Claims about practical success are weaker because Myanmar remained unwilling, the repatriation environment was not conducive, and much of the record is retrospective synthesis rather than direct operational documentation.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Rohingya repatriation stayed on the diplomatic track**
- Claim: In 2023 Bangladesh and Myanmar continued a bilateral pilot repatriation effort for Rohingya refugees, with UNHCR observing that the agency was not party to the talks and that conditions in Myanmar’s Rakhine State were not conducive to safe, sustainable return.
- Why it matters: This is the central 2B peace-and-settlement issue for Bangladesh in 2023.
- Strong sources:
  - UNHCR statement on the pilot project, 19 Mar. 2023: [UNHCR Myanmar](https://myanmar.un.org/en/223904-unhcr-statement-bangladesh-myanmar-bilateral-pilot-project-rohingya-returns)
  - Reuters report on the May 2023 pilot visit: [Reuters via ThePrint](https://theprint.in/world/rohingya-bangladesh-officials-to-visit-myanmar-in-repatriation-effort/1555232/)
  - Bangladesh UN mission statement, 15 Nov. 2023: [Bangladesh UN Mission](https://nypm.mofa.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6952667d35ce18e1c05a9829)
- Locator:
  - UNHCR: UNHCR says it is aware of the bilateral pilot project, is not involved, and considers conditions in Myanmar not conducive.
  - Reuters: “go and see” visit to Rakhine by Rohingya and Bangladeshi officials.
  - Bangladesh UN Mission: Bangladesh says it opened its border for nearly a million Rohingya fleeing ethnic cleansing and thanks delegations for support on the Myanmar resolution.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for the existence of diplomacy; moderate for effectiveness.
- Cautions: Repatriation remained aspirational, and UNHCR explicitly says conditions were not suitable.

2. **Bangladesh publicly framed the Rohingya issue as a lawful humanitarian and diplomatic matter**
- Claim: In 2023 Bangladesh continued to present the Rohingya question at the UN as a humanitarian and legal problem requiring international burden-sharing and eventual voluntary return, not as a military issue.
- Why it matters: This bears on 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, and 2B.10.
- Strong sources:
  - UN General Assembly / Human Rights Council coverage on Rohingya resolution: [UNOG newsroom](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1720/hrc-53-resolution-on-human-rights-of-rohingya-in-myanmar-14-july-2023)
  - Bangladesh statement at the UN, 15 Nov. 2023: [Bangladesh UN Mission](https://nypm.mofa.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/6952667d35ce18e1c05a9829)
  - UN Secretary-General statement on the Rohingya anniversary, 25 Aug. 2023: [UN Press](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sgsm21913.doc.htm)
- Locator:
  - UNOG: HRC resolution calls on Myanmar to end violence, cooperate with UN mechanisms, and create conditions for voluntary, safe, dignified return; it also encourages support to Bangladesh.
  - Bangladesh UN Mission: 15 Nov. statement on Rohingya resolution by consensus.
  - UN Press: SG calls for comprehensive solutions and acknowledges Bangladesh’s humanitarian commitments.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for rhetoric and formal diplomacy.
- Cautions: These are diplomatic positions, not proof of implementation success.

3. **Bangladesh remained a major UN peacekeeping contributor**
- Claim: In 2023 Bangladesh remained the world’s largest contributor of uniformed personnel to UN peacekeeping operations, with more than 7,400 personnel deployed in 14 countries.
- Why it matters: This is the strongest positive evidence for lawful international-security engagement in the period.
- Strong source: UN Resident Coordinator speech in Dhaka, 29 May 2023: [UN Bangladesh speech](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/234140-speech-ms-gwyn-lewis-un-resident-coordinator-bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - Lines 83-85 on Bangladesh’s “unwavering commitment” and scale of deployments.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for UN-recognized contribution.
- Cautions: Peacekeeping contribution is not the same as conflict restraint in bilateral disputes, but it is relevant to lawful security posture.

4. **Bangladesh’s peacekeeping posture included gender participation and training infrastructure**
- Claim: Bangladesh pledged to increase female troop participation and pointed to BIPSOT as a cornerstone of peacekeeping preparation.
- Why it matters: This shows institutionalization and some professionalization of lawful security policy.
- Strong source: [UN Bangladesh speech](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/234140-speech-ms-gwyn-lewis-un-resident-coordinator-bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - Lines 84-94 on female peacekeepers, National Action Plan on Women Peace and Security, and BIPSOT.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: This is a UN speech praising Bangladesh; it is not an audit of operational performance.

5. **UNHCR’s 2023 response plan shows the scale of Bangladesh’s refugee-security burden**
- Claim: The 2023 Joint Response Plan covered January-December 2023 for Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh, including Kutupalong, Nayapara, and Bhasan Char.
- Why it matters: It establishes the persistent crisis that shaped Bangladesh’s international security and humanitarian choices.
- Strong source: [JRP 2023](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/111227)
- Locator:
  - Document title and description lines 22-28.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for baseline humanitarian exposure.
- Cautions: This is a response-plan index page, not a detailed operational assessment.

6. **The 2023 JRP report confirms the crisis persisted through the year**
- Claim: The companion JRP report covers January-December 2023 and documents the continuing Rohingya response architecture.
- Why it matters: It supports the claim that the issue remained active throughout 2023 rather than being a one-off event.
- Strong source: [JRP Report 2023](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/111233)
- Locator:
  - Document title and description lines 23-29.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for chronology.
- Cautions: The page itself is only a portal entry; the PDF would be needed for richer operational detail.

7. **Bangladesh’s refugee posture was defensive and humanitarian, not militarized**
- Claim: UNHCR describes Rohingya refugees in Bangladesh as depending entirely on humanitarian assistance and living in camps and Bhasan Char, while UNHCR supports safe, voluntary, dignified, and sustainable return when conditions in Myanmar permit.
- Why it matters: This is the clearest contextual evidence that Bangladesh’s conflict exposure in 2023 was managed through humanitarian administration, not offensive action.
- Strong source: [UNHCR Bangladesh page](https://www.unhcr.org/where-we-work/countries/bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - Lines 402-404 on one million Rohingya in Cox’s Bazar and Bhasan Char, aid dependence, and the return principle.
- Period fit: Current operational overview with direct relevance to 2023 conditions.
- Attribution: Strong for the refugee context.
- Cautions: UNHCR is describing the situation from an operational perspective and does not assign blame for all underlying causes.

**Lens Disposition**

- **2B.1 Peaceful alternatives:** Supported. The government pursued talks and a pilot repatriation process rather than force.
- **2B.10 Lasting international peace:** Partial. Bangladesh remained on a diplomatic, lawful track, but the core Myanmar problem was unresolved.
- **2B.2 Starting or prolonging war:** No evidence found. Bangladesh was not initiating war in 2023.
- **2B.3 Honest justification for force:** Largely not applicable; no force case was found. For the Rohingya file, official justifications were humanitarian and diplomatic.
- **2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection:** Partial. The record is about civilian protection for refugees, not battlefield prisoner treatment.
- **2B.5 Control of proxies and arms:** No evidence found in the inspected 2023 record.
- **2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims:** Partial. The UN process and public diplomacy were visible; no separate domestic scrutiny mechanism of a war claim was found because there was no active war claim.
- **2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements:** Partial. Bangladesh pursued repatriation arrangements, but no durable settlement was achieved.
- **2B.8 Responsible military resources:** Partial. Peacekeeping and training are positive, but I did not build a full military-budget record for this chapter.
- **2B.9 Accountability and remedy:** Partial to weak. I found no 2023 conflict accountability process beyond humanitarian diplomacy and UN advocacy.

**Source Groups**

- [UNHCR JRP Report 2023](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/111233)

**Corroboration sources**
- [UNHCR Myanmar/Rohingya statements and operational pages](https://myanmar.un.org/en/223904-unhcr-statement-bangladesh-myanmar-bilateral-pilot-project-rohingya-returns)
- [UN General Assembly / Human Rights Council coverage on Rohingya](https://www.unognewsroom.org/story/en/1720/hrc-53-resolution-on-human-rights-of-rohingya-in-myanmar-14-july-2023)
- [UN Press on Rohingya anniversary](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sgsm21913.doc.htm)

**Uninspected leads**
- The full PDF behind the 2023 JRP and 2023 JRP Report pages.
- Bangladesh UN mission statements on Myanmar resolution and ICC-related matters.
- ACLED Rohingya-border or election-security material if it turns up a cross-border or coercive-security angle.
- UK House of Commons Library note on Rohingya support.
- Additional Reuters coverage on bilateral repatriation and Myanmar border conditions.
- ICRC or UNHCR narrative reporting on border crossings, maritime movements, and camp security.

**Research gaps**
- I did not find evidence of an interstate war, proxy conflict, or coercive military operation by Bangladesh in 2023.
- The main unresolved question is whether the repatriation diplomacy had any measurable operational effect beyond keeping the issue alive.
- I did not assemble a military-budget or procurement record for 2B.8; that would require a separate resource pass.
- I also did not find a concrete 2023 accountability or remedy process tied to use of force, because the record did not show a direct war episode.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

**Orientation**

Bangladesh’s 2023 domestic safety record under Sheikh Hasina is dominated by state coercion, not ordinary crime control. The strongest evidence shows continued enforced disappearances, custodial abuse, arbitrary detention, and repression of dissent, with security institutions used against political opponents, journalists, labor organizers, and some minority communities. The government did not establish credible independent accountability mechanisms, and when it did acknowledge abuses it usually did so selectively and without transparent remedy.

The clearest positive evidence is narrower: the state did maintain formal legal prohibitions on torture, had some internal police review procedures, and allowed limited prison inspection and civil-society monitoring. But the operational record does not show those safeguards working effectively. Information quality is mixed but usable: U.S. State Department, HRW, Amnesty, ARTICLE 19, OHCHR, and RSF/CIVICUS give a consistent picture, while official sources mainly help with formal law, official explanations, or self-justification.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Security forces committed killings, disappearances, and torture; accountability was weak**
- Fact: In 2023, Bangladesh’s security forces were credibly reported to have committed arbitrary killings, enforced disappearances, torture, and harsh detention abuses, while the government failed to release full statistics or provide transparent investigations and meaningful punishment.
- Why it matters: This is the core 3B.1 and 3B.2 record.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report via ecoi.net, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Executive summary and Section 1.A-C, especially lines 37-49, 54-66.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong. The report attributes the abuses to government agents and security forces.
- Cautions: The report is secondary, but it synthesizes a wide range of contemporary reporting and local monitors.
- Corroboration: HRW, Amnesty, and UN special-procedure communications.

2. **Extrajudicial killings continued, including notable custody and RAB cases**
- Fact: The report describes deaths in custody and suspected extrajudicial killings, including the March 2023 death of Sultana Jasmine in RAB custody and the shooting death in Sonargaon, with official explanations challenged by witnesses and courts.
- Why it matters: It gives concrete incident-level evidence for unlawful killing and impunity.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Lines 45-52.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong. The incidents involved state security actors.
- Cautions: Some details remain contested because official investigations were not public.

3. **Enforced disappearances remained a live pattern, mostly affecting opposition and dissidents**
- Fact: Disappearances continued in 2023; the report says local monitors counted 32 victims from January to September, and most victims were opposition leaders, activists, or dissidents.
- Why it matters: This is central to restraint/protection and state abuse.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Lines 53-60.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The government disputed civil-society counts and said it was looking into some cases.

4. **Torture remained systemic, especially in remand and custody**
- Fact: Security forces, including intelligence services, police, and soldiers seconded into civilian law enforcement, were reported to use torture; remand custody created conditions where abuse could occur without counsel.
- Why it matters: This is the main 3B.1 and 3B.4 issue.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Lines 61-68.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Torture claims are hard to document because victims and lawyers face retaliation and prison grievance systems were weak.

5. **Prisons were overcrowded, harsh, and lacked meaningful complaint remedies**
- Fact: Prison capacity was far below the number of prisoners; prisons lacked a formal complaint process and had no ombudsperson, while credible allegations were rarely investigated.
- Why it matters: This is part of 3B.1 and 3B.4, showing a weak protection system.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Lines 72-80.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The report relies partly on prison administration figures and monitoring access that was itself limited.

6. **Arbitrary arrest was broad and often tied to political demonstrations and speech**
- Fact: The report says arbitrary arrests occurred in conjunction with political demonstrations and speech, and that authorities used broad legal exceptions and rearrest practices.
- Why it matters: This is a key 3B.5 and 3B.8 finding.
- Strongest source: U.S. State Department 2023 Human Rights Report, [link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107613.html)
- Locator: Lines 81-91.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Legal text alone would not prove abuse; here the report combines text and practice.

7. **The government replaced the Digital Security Act with the Cyber Security Act, but rights groups said the repression largely continued**
- Fact: In 2023 the government replaced the DSA with the CSA, but ARTICLE 19 and HRW both say many abusive elements remained and the law continued to be used against journalists and critics.
- Why it matters: This is the main formal-law and emergency/surveillance-power record for 3B.5 and 3B.8.
- Strongest sources:
  - ARTICLE 19, [link](https://www.article19.org/resources/bangladesh-defending-free-expression-and-electoral-integrity-in-the-digital-age/)
  - HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh, [link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - ARTICLE 19 lines 69-72.
  - HRW lines 58-60, 75-77.
- Period fit: Directly 2023, with retrospective confirmation in 2024.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The government argued the new law reduced penalties and arrest risks; that claim needs testing against enforcement.

8. **The state’s public messaging on cyber law was defensive and framed the change as reassurance, not liberalization**
- Fact: The law minister said in August 2023 that the new cyber law would remove journalists’ anxieties and reduce some punishments, but also explicitly said the DSA was being changed rather than truly abolished.
- Why it matters: This is useful official rhetoric for 3B.3, 3B.5, and 3B.8.
- Strongest source: BSS report of the law minister’s briefing, [link](https://www.bssnews.net/bangla/news-flash/101894)
- Locator: Lines 56-67.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for what the government said.
- Cautions: State media reporting is not independent verification and may sanitize criticism.

9. **Press freedom remained badly constrained, with journalists facing arrest, legal harassment, and self-censorship**
- Fact: ARTICLE 19 says freedom of expression was heavily repressed from 2018 through 2023, with hundreds of cases under the DSA and major chilling effects on journalism.
- Why it matters: Press repression is a major source of fear and weakens all domestic-safety oversight.
- Strongest source: ARTICLE 19, [link](https://www.article19.org/resources/bangladesh-defending-free-expression-and-electoral-integrity-in-the-digital-age/)
- Locator: Lines 69-72.
- Period fit: Mix of inherited and 2023 target period.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The counts span multiple years, so they are not pure 2023-only evidence.

10. **The pre-election period featured mass arrests and politically motivated pressure on opposition members**
- Fact: HRW says 2023 saw tightened repression ahead of national elections, including mass arrests of opposition members and excessive force against protests.
- Why it matters: This is the best concise 3B.8 and 3B.10 framing for the electoral-security environment.
- Strongest source: HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh, [link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 56-60.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: It is a summary chapter, not a case file.

11. **The government declined a UN-supported independent mechanism for disappearance cases**
- Fact: HRW says Bangladesh refused the UN offer to help establish a specialized mechanism to investigate enforced disappearances.
- Why it matters: This is central to 3B.4 accountability and remedy.
- Strongest source: HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh, [link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 61-63.
- Period fit: Retrospective on 2023 conditions.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The refusal is an after-the-fact summary of an earlier UN offer; confirm with the underlying UN correspondence if this becomes score-bearing.

12. **Bangladesh’s information environment was too constrained for clean domestic oversight**
- Fact: HRW and ARTICLE 19 both describe self-censorship, harassment of journalists, and legal targeting of critics; this made independent scrutiny of abuses difficult.
- Why it matters: This affects the reliability of all domestic safety claims.
- Strongest sources:
  - HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh, [link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
  - ARTICLE 19, [link](https://www.article19.org/resources/bangladesh-defending-free-expression-and-electoral-integrity-in-the-digital-age/)
- Locator:
  - HRW lines 69-77.
  - ARTICLE 19 lines 69-72.
- Period fit: Directly 2023 and immediate aftermath.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: This is an evidence-quality finding as much as a rights finding.

13. **Indigenous Bawm villagers in the Chittagong Hill Tracts faced arbitrary arrest under security framing**
- Fact: Amnesty later reported that in April-May 2024 over 100 Indigenous Bawm people were arbitrarily arrested in an ongoing military operation in the CHT; this is later-retrospective but indicates a continuity problem in the domestic-safety file.
- Why it matters: Useful as a later check on state protection of vulnerable groups.
- Strongest source: Amnesty International Bangladesh country page, [link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/asia-and-the-pacific/south-asia/bangladesh/report-bangladesh/)
- Locator: Lines 99-102.
- Period fit: Later-retrospective, not pure 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: This is outside the target year and should not be overused.

14. **Rohingya camps remained insecure and coercive**
- Fact: HRW says refugees in the camps faced threats, extortion, arbitrary arrests, and torture by the Armed Police Battalion; Amnesty says the camps remained hostile and rights-poor.
- Why it matters: This shows domestic safety failures affecting a large vulnerable population under Bangladeshi control.
- Strongest sources:
  - HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh, [link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
  - Amnesty International Bangladesh page, [link](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/asia-and-the-pacific/south-asia/bangladesh/report-bangladesh/)
- Locator:
  - HRW lines 609-610.
  - Amnesty lines 55-56.
- Period fit: 2023 and later-retrospective.
- Attribution: Strong enough to support the protection record.
- Cautions: Much of this is refugee-camp governance, not general-population safety.

**Lens Disposition**

- **3B.1 Protection from state abuse:** Strongly adverse. The evidence shows repeated torture, disappearances, arbitrary killing, and weak redress.
- **3B.10 Lasting domestic safety:** Adverse. There was no durable improvement in protection from state violence; the repression environment worsened around elections.
- **3B.2 Control of coercive forces:** Adverse. Security forces were not credibly disciplined at scale, and impunity was widespread.
- **3B.3 Incitement and targeted hatred:** Partial/adverse. I found official attacks on journalists and opposition rather than a clean record of restraint.
- **3B.4 Complaints and independent oversight:** Adverse. Oversight and remedy were weak, prison complaints systems were poor, and UN-backed mechanisms were rejected.
- **3B.5 Emergency and surveillance powers:** Adverse. Broad security laws were used to intimidate critics; the CSA did not end the repressive pattern.
- **3B.6 Safety without state terror:** Adverse. Any reductions in some crime types were overshadowed by state terror and fear.
- **3B.7 Protection of vulnerable groups:** Adverse. Rohingya, Indigenous people, and some labor groups remained exposed to coercion and abuse.
- **3B.8 Safe protest and dissent:** Adverse. Protesters, journalists, and opposition figures faced violent dispersal, arrests, and legal harassment.
- **3B.9 Restrained crisis response:** Adverse. The 2023 protest and security response relied on force, internet restrictions, and legal pressure rather than restraint.

**Uninspected leads**
- CPJ Bangladesh DSA/CSA reports.
- The full text of the UN special rapporteur communication on Bangladesh’s freedom of expression file.
- The 2023 Committee Against Torture follow-up materials specific to Bangladesh.
- The full Amnesty report PDF for 2024/25 if you want post-2023 continuity on protests and repression.
- The CHT-specific OHCHR communication and related reports if you want a deeper vulnerable-groups file.
- The World Bank project on transparency/accountability, if you want institutional reform context.

**Research gaps**
- I did not build a complete formal-law record for the Special Powers Act or the Cyber Security Act beyond their documented use and official defense.
- I did not extract a full prison-audit trail or case-level judicial record for all custody deaths and disappearances.
- I did not fully separate local policing from national command in every protest episode, so some 3B.8 evidence remains partly attributable through policy/tolerance rather than direct order.
- I did not find any credible evidence that Hasina’s government reduced physical-integrity abuse in 2023; the record points the other way.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

**Orientation**

Bangladesh in 2023 was not a competitive democracy in the strong sense. Sheikh Hasina’s government controlled the executive, the ruling Awami League dominated institutions, and the principal pattern in the political-freedom record was institutional entrenchment plus repression of opposition, journalists, and civic actors. The 2024 election cycle was already in motion during 2023, so the best evidence for this chapter is a mix of contemporaneous reporting and 2024 retrospective assessments of the 2023 environment.

The strongest record shows: a tightly managed electoral field, heavy use of cyber and speech laws, intimidation of critics, weak independent checks, and a public-information environment that made scrutiny difficult. I did not find credible evidence in the inspected sources of meaningful democratic opening in 2023. Limited positive items include the existence of formal constitutional and electoral structures, but the operational record does not show those structures constraining the ruling party in a durable way.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Dominant-party control and weak checks**
- Claim: By 2023 the Awami League had consolidated power, and Parliament and other institutions provided weak checks on the executive.
- Why it matters: This is the baseline for every 4B lens.
- Strong source: [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2024: Bangladesh](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024)
- Locator: Overview and C2 discussion.
- Period fit: Retrospective on 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Evaluative summary, not a case file.

2. **Opposition boycott pressure and coercive election environment**
- Claim: In late 2023 the BNP said it would boycott the election unless Hasina stepped aside, amid arrests, prosecutions, and intimidation of opposition leaders.
- Why it matters: This is direct evidence on genuine electoral choice and entrenched power.
- Strong source: [Reuters via ThePrint, 1 Nov. 2023](https://theprint.in/world/bangladeshs-main-opposition-to-boycott-vote-if-hasina-stays-put/1827169/)
- Locator: Lead paragraphs.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Reuters is reporting the situation, not adjudicating every allegation.

3. **Cyber Security Act preserved coercive digital control**
- Claim: Bangladesh enacted the Cyber Security Act, 2023, which retained broad state powers over search, seizure, and arrest in cyber cases.
- Why it matters: This is the central formal-law item for digital repression and political control.
- Strong source: [Cyber Security Act, 2023](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-print-1457/section-print-52866.html)
- Locator: Section 42 on warrantless search, seizure, and arrest.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for formal law.
- Cautions: The statute text shows powers, not actual use.

4. **Government framed the cyber-law change as reassurance, not liberalization**
- Claim: The law minister publicly said the new cyber law would reduce journalists’ anxieties and replace the prior law rather than end the coercive system.
- Why it matters: This is useful rhetoric for how the government justified continued control.
- Strong source: [BSS report, Aug. 2023](https://www.bssnews.net/bangla/news-flash/101894)
- Locator: Report text quoting the law minister.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: State media source; use as official rhetoric, not neutral validation.

5. **Free expression remained heavily repressed**
- Claim: ARTICLE 19 said freedom of expression in Bangladesh had been heavily repressed from 2018 through 2023, with extensive DSA cases and continuing repressive effects under the 2023 Cyber Security Act.
- Why it matters: This is one of the clearest records for opposition, journalist, and civic-space suppression.
- Strong source: [ARTICLE 19](https://www.article19.org/resources/bangladesh-defending-free-expression-and-electoral-integrity-in-the-digital-age/)
- Locator: Lines 69-72 and 91-99.
- Period fit: Mix of inherited and target-period evidence.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Later-retrospective synthesis, but highly relevant.

6. **Protesters faced deaths, arrests, and repression**
- Claim: Amnesty said Bangladesh’s 2023 protest response involved repeated deaths, arrests, and repression.
- Why it matters: This is direct evidence for unsafe protest and retaliatory policing.
- Strong source: [Amnesty International, 31 Oct. 2023](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/10/bangladesh-repeated-cycle-of-deaths-arrests-and-repression-during-protests-must-end)
- Locator: Press release body.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Advocacy format, so it should be corroborated with other sources, which it is.

7. **The information environment was restricted**
- Claim: Freedom House’s country profile describes Bangladesh’s civic and political environment as restricted, which helps explain weak scrutiny of government conduct in 2023.
- Why it matters: This affects how much trust to place in official claims and why abuse could persist.
- Strong source: [Freedom House country profile](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh)
- Locator: Country overview.
- Period fit: Retrospective context.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Broad context rather than incident-specific proof.

8. **Independent scrutiny of repression was already being raised by UN-linked bodies**
- Claim: OHCHR special-procedures material records Bangladesh-related communications concerning repression and civic space.
- Why it matters: This supports the conclusion that political-freedom concerns were internationalized and not merely local polemics.
- Strong source: [OHCHR special procedures search](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/Mandates?m=31&sort=country)
- Locator: Bangladesh communications index.
- Period fit: Mixed, but relevant to the 2023 environment.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Index pages require follow-on document inspection for case-level detail.

9. **The election environment was already politically violent**
- Claim: ACLED and CIVICUS materials described a pre-election crackdown and rising political violence.
- Why it matters: This ties coercion to the 4B.1 and 4B.8 election-quality questions.
- Strong sources:
  - [CIVICUS Monitor: Bangladesh](https://monitor.civicus.org/country/bangladesh)
  - [CIVICUS watchlist, Sept. 2023](https://monitor.civicus.org/watchlist-september-2023/BangladeshWL)
  - [ACLED: Bangladesh heads toward a landmark election amid rising political violence](https://acleddata.com/report/bangladesh-heads-toward-landmark-election-amid-rising-political-violence)
- Locator: Opening summaries and political-violence framing.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong to moderate.
- Cautions: Some pieces are retrospective or summary-heavy.

10. **Courts, election bodies, and local institutions did not clearly check the executive**
- Claim: The record does not show independent institutions constraining Hasina in a way that altered the political trajectory in 2023.
- Why it matters: This is the negative finding for 4B.4 and 4B.5.
- Strong sources:
  - [Freedom House 2024](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024)
  - [World Bank transparency/accountability project](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099052325123061621)
  - [Bangladesh Election Commission archive](https://www.ecs.gov.bd/category/presidential-election)
- Locator: Institutional-weakness discussion and EC archive context.
- Period fit: Mostly 2023, partly retrospective.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: The absence of evidence is weaker than direct evidence of capture, but the broader record is consistent.

11. **Digital repression had a chilling effect on electoral integrity**
- Claim: ARTICLE 19 links the digital legal regime to electoral integrity and free-expression suppression.
- Why it matters: This combines 4B.6 and 4B.9.
- Strong source: [ARTICLE 19](https://www.article19.org/resources/bangladesh-defending-free-expression-and-electoral-integrity-in-the-digital-age/)
- Locator: Discussion of law and 2023 election environment.
- Period fit: Target-period and later-retrospective.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Long-form synthesis; line-specific factual claims should be cross-checked if used in score-bearing work.

12. **Hasina’s rule did not leave a stronger democratic inheritance**
- Claim: The balance of evidence points to a weaker, more restricted political order at the end of 2023 than the democratic ideal implied by formal institutions.
- Why it matters: This is the best available answer for 4B.10.
- Strong sources: [Freedom House 2024](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024), [HRW World Report 2024 Bangladesh](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh), [Amnesty country page](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/asia-and-the-pacific/south-asia/bangladesh/report-bangladesh/)
- Locator: Country chapters and overview sections.
- Period fit: Retrospective on 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: This is an inference from multiple sources, not a single explicit statement.

**Lens Disposition**

- **4B.1 Genuine electoral choice:** Adverse. The 2023 record shows a coercive, opposition-weakening election environment.
- **4B.10 Lasting democratic resilience:** Adverse. The evidence points to further weakening, not strengthening, of political freedom.
- **4B.2 No entrenchment of power:** Adverse. The ruling party used institutions and law to entrench itself.
- **4B.3 Opposition and civic freedom:** Adverse. Opposition, journalists, and civic groups faced arrests, intimidation, and legal pressure.
- **4B.4 Independent checks on power:** Adverse. No convincing check on executive dominance is visible in the 2023 record.
- **4B.5 Politically neutral institutions:** Adverse. The record points toward politicized institutions and loyalty pressure.
- **4B.6 Independent information and media:** Adverse. The legal and operational environment was hostile to independent information.
- **4B.7 Equal political rights:** Partial/adverse. Formal equality existed on paper; in practice, excluded groups and dissenting voices faced unequal access and protection.
- **4B.8 Term limits and transfer:** Adverse. The 2023 record reflects continued power retention, not credible turnover.
- **4B.9 Digital freedom and surveillance:** Adverse. The Cyber Security Act and related enforcement were used to suppress politics.

- The full OHCHR special-procedures Bangladesh communication texts.
- CPJ’s Bangladesh DSA/CSA coverage.
- The Bangladesh Election Commission archive materials on the 2024 election and electoral administration.
- The economic journal article on partisan effects of information campaigns in Bangladesh elections.
- The detailed ACLED election briefs for event-level violence counts.
- Any court or election-commission records on specific opposition disqualifications or candidate exclusions.

**Research Gaps**
- I did not build a full case-level ledger of every arrest or protest incident.
- I did not exhaust the election-commission and court records for formal exclusion/disqualification evidence.
- I did not separately quantify media ownership, broadcast licensing, or internet restrictions beyond the cyber-law and rights-report record.
- I did not find credible evidence of a 2023 democratic reform breakthrough; the available evidence points the other way.
