--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

**Orientation**

Bangladesh’s 2023 economic record under Sheikh Hasina is mixed but not a collapse story. The country still had strong medium-term growth fundamentals and large gains in electrification, education access, and social protection coverage, but 2023 itself was a year of macro stress: inflation stayed elevated, foreign exchange reserves and the taka came under pressure, imports were compressed, and growth slowed. The government’s own budget and social-protection claims show active spending and reform language, but the strongest independent sources say the macro stance was strained and the banking/FX environment was fragile.

What matters for this chapter is not GDP alone. The best evidence points to: slower growth than the previous decade, rising poverty risk, weaker inclusion, heavy dependence on garments and remittances, and a policy response that leaned on IMF support, monetary tightening, and gradual reform rather than a clean stabilization success.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Macro stress and slower growth in FY23**
- Fact: Bangladesh’s post-pandemic recovery weakened in FY23; the IMF said real GDP growth slowed to about 6 percent, headline inflation reached 9.9 percent in August 2023, the current-account deficit narrowed under import compression, and reserves and the taka remained under pressure.
- Why it matters: This is the core macroeconomic condition for 5B.3, 5B.9, and 5B.10.
- Strongest source: IMF press release, 12 Dec. 2023, [IMF](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/12/12/pr23440-bangladesh-imf-exec-board-concludes-2023-art-iv-consult-completes-1st-rev-ecf-eef-rsf)
- Locator: Lines 219-222.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong, though global shocks are a major confounder.
- Cautions: IMF is strongest on macro aggregates; household experience needs separate evidence.

2. **World Bank said FY23 recovery was disrupted by inflation, external pressure, and financial-sector vulnerabilities**
- Fact: The World Bank’s October 2023 update said recovery from COVID was disrupted in FY23 by rising inflation, financial-sector vulnerabilities, external pressure, and global uncertainty; it urged monetary and fiscal reforms and a more market-based exchange rate.
- Why it matters: This is the best concise official synthesis of the 2023 macro environment.
- Strongest source: World Bank press release, 3 Oct. 2023, [World Bank](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/10/02/strong-reforms-are-critical-for-sustaining-growth-in-bangladesh-world-bank-expects-solid-growth-but-risky-outlook-for-so)
- Locator: Description paragraphs in the press release.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: This is a synthesis and policy recommendation, not an audit.

3. **Bangladesh Bank’s own annual-report metadata shows an inflation/reserves/exchange-rate management file**
- Fact: Bangladesh Bank’s FY23 annual report and open data infrastructure indicate that official macro management centered on inflation, reserve trends, exchange rates, and balance-of-payments pressures.
- Why it matters: This is the state-side institutional frame for 5B.3 and 5B.7.
- Strongest source: Bangladesh Bank annual-report archive and open-data page, [Annual Report archive](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/publication/publictn/0/2), [Open data](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/econdata/index), [MPS archive](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/monetaryactivity/monetarypolicy)
- Locator: FY 2022-23 annual report listing; open-data description of monthly economic trends; MPS archive entries.
- Period fit: Direct and inherited.
- Attribution: Strong for state priorities, weaker for performance.
- Cautions: I could not open the PDF cleanly here, so this is more structural than statistical.

4. **The central bank’s policy stance was explicitly tied to price stability and growth**
- Fact: Bangladesh Bank’s monetary-policy archive says monetary policy is intended to support faster inclusive growth and poverty reduction while maintaining price stability.
- Why it matters: This is the formal policy framework behind 5B.3 and 5B.9.
- Strongest source: Bangladesh Bank monetary-policy archive, [Bangladesh Bank](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/monetaryactivity/monetarypolicy)
- Locator: Archive intro and FY 2023 entries.
- Period fit: Inherited framework active in 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for formal policy intent.
- Cautions: Intent is not execution.

5. **FX and external-balance pressure were severe enough to force policy changes**
- Fact: IMF staff said frequent exchange-rate-policy changes, FX uncertainty, and expectations of depreciation contributed to financial outflows; Bangladesh Bank in March 2023 mandated conversion of export receipts at the market rate prevailing when proceeds were due.
- Why it matters: This is specific evidence of policy correction under stress.
- Strongest source: IMF staff report snippet, 2023, [IMF staff country report](https://www.elibrary.imf.org/view/journals/002/2023/410/article-A002-en.xml)
- Locator: Paragraph 4 in the snippet shown in search results.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for policy response, moderate for effectiveness.
- Cautions: IMF staff analysis is technical and focused on FX mechanics, not broader prosperity.

6. **2023 ended with larger reserve coverage than early-2023, but only after compression and adjustment**
- Fact: Bangladesh Bank annual-data snippets show gross official reserves were under stress in the period, while remittance inflows and export receipts were critical stabilizers.
- Why it matters: This is part of the balance-of-payments and resilience story for 5B.3 and 5B.9.
- Strongest source: Bangladesh Bank BOP annual data, [BB BOP annual data](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/econdata/bop_yearly/1)
- Locator: Annual-data table in the snippet.
- Period fit: The snippet itself is FY24/FY25, but it contextualizes the reserve-management path out of FY23 stress.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Because the snippet is later-year data, I use it only as background context, not as a direct FY23 claim.

7. **Remittances remained a major household-support channel**
- Fact: Bangladesh Bank data show large annual remittance inflows, with the system heavily reliant on a few labor-destination countries and banks.
- Why it matters: This is central to household welfare and shock absorption.
- Strongest source: Bangladesh Bank remittance data, [BB remittances](https://www.bb.org.bd/en/index.php/econdata/wageremitance)
- Locator: Monthly and annual tables in the BB data page.
- Period fit: The page is current, but the 2023-24 monthly series and 2022-23 comparison are directly relevant to the FY23/FY24 transition.
- Attribution: Strong for remittance flows; weaker for causation.
- Cautions: This is a support mechanism, not a government achievement by itself.

8. **Poverty reduction slowed and vulnerability remained high**
- Fact: World Bank poverty work says Bangladesh reduced poverty substantially over the long run, but the pace slowed after 2016 and more than one-third of the population remained vulnerable to falling back into poverty; the 2023 update said reform was needed to sustain poverty reduction.
- Why it matters: This is the best distributional record for 5B.8 and 5B.10.
- Strongest source: World Bank press release on the 2025 poverty assessment, [World Bank](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2025/11/25/job-creation-key-to-equality-and-faster-poverty-reduction-in-bangladesh-says-world-bank) and the report summary, [World Bank](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099112625114013973)
- Locator: Press-release summary of the assessment; report metadata and summary.
- Period fit: Retrospective on 2010-2022, useful baseline for 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: This is not a 2023-only welfare snapshot, but it anchors the inherited trajectory.

9. **Living standards improved on some basic-services measures**
- Fact: World Bank country material says nearly all households have electricity, life expectancy rose above 70, and Bangladesh made major gains in health, education, sanitation, and social protection.
- Why it matters: This is the strongest pro-prosperity evidence for 5B.1 and 5B.6.
- Strongest source: World Bank Bangladesh country page, [World Bank](https://www.worldbank.org/ext/en/country/bangladesh)
- Locator: About, Economy, and Results sections.
- Period fit: Mostly inherited and medium-term, not a pure 2023 outcome.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: These are broad development gains, not a verdict on 2023 policy quality.

10. **Public investment and social protection were sizable, but the state framed them as tools of political legitimacy too**
- Fact: The FY24 budget was large, and government messaging emphasized social safety nets, universal pension, and coverage for vulnerable groups.
- Why it matters: This is central to 5B.1, 5B.6, and 5B.8.
- Strong source: BSS budget reporting, 20 June 2023, [BSS](https://www.bssnews.net/news/132401)
- Locator: Paragraph on Tk 1.26 lakh crore for the social safety net and the universal pension scheme.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for what the government said and budgeted.
- Cautions: State media, and the claim is about allocation rather than actual delivery.

11. **The FY24 budget was formally passed and described as welfare-oriented**
- Fact: Parliament passed the 2023-24 budget after debate.
- Why it matters: This supports the formal side of 5B.1 and 5B.6, though not the quality of execution.
- Strong source: BSS report on the budget session, 6 July 2023, [BSS](https://www.bssnews.net/bangla/news-flash/97212)
- Locator: Paragraph describing the 7.61 trillion taka budget and passage on 26 June 2023.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for parliamentary passage.
- Cautions: A passed budget is not evidence of effective implementation.

12. **Government revenue and budget portals existed, but the data trail needs fuller extraction**
- Fact: Ministry of Finance, NBR, and cabinet budget archives show that budget, revision, and revenue documents were published and tracked through 2023.
- Why it matters: This is the documentary base for future work on 5B.3, 5B.5, and 5B.7.
- Strong sources:
  - [Finance Ministry budget archive](https://mof.gov.bd/site/page/591b7120-945c-4b88-9bd5-3447963f6249)
  - [NBR budget portal](https://nbr.gov.bd/budget/budget/eng/ban)
  - [Cabinet budget archive](https://cabinet.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/694032d635ce18e1c056333e)
- Locator: FY 2023-24 archive entries.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for formal recordkeeping.
- Cautions: I did not extract the underlying PDF tables here.

**Lens Disposition**

- **5B.1 Broad and sustainable prosperity:** Partial support. The government budgeted and spoke in developmental terms, but the record also shows fragility and likely political use of spending.
- **5B.10 Lasting economic trajectory:** Mixed. Bangladesh retained long-run gains, but 2023 was a year of weaker momentum and higher vulnerability.
- **5B.2 Qualified economic leadership:** Partial. The record shows established central-bank and finance institutions, but I did not yet isolate appointment quality or independence in detail.
- **5B.3 Macroeconomic stability:** Adverse/mixed. The government and central bank tried to stabilize the economy, but inflation, reserves, and FX pressure were severe.
- **5B.4 Fair economic rules:** Partial. Formal rules existed, but the record here is not enough to conclude they were consistently fair in practice.
- **5B.5 Resistance to economic capture:** Partial to weak. I did not build enough capture-specific evidence in this pass.
- **5B.6 Productive public investment:** Partial. Spending on social safety nets, energy, education, and infrastructure was substantial, but execution quality needs more work.
- **5B.7 Evidence and correction:** Mixed. The IMF and World Bank show policy correction under pressure, but not a consistently evidence-led regime.
- **5B.8 Fair distribution:** Mixed/adverse. Basic services improved over time, but vulnerability, inflation, and slowing poverty reduction indicate uneven distribution.
- **5B.9 Managing economic shocks:** Partial. The authorities did respond with IMF support, tighter policy, and FX adjustments, but the shock response was reactive and constrained.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

**Orientation**

Bangladesh’s 2023 social-well-being record under Sheikh Hasina is best described as a mixed development trajectory under macro stress. The country had already achieved major long-run gains in health, schooling, electricity access, and poverty reduction, but in 2023 those gains were under pressure from inflation, food insecurity, disaster exposure, and weak service quality in vulnerable areas. The strongest evidence shows that the government and its partners continued to fund and run large social-protection, nutrition, education, and disaster-response systems, but delivery remained uneven and many households were still exposed to malnutrition, poverty vulnerability, and crisis shocks.

The central distinction is between inherited long-run progress and 2023 execution. The long-run picture is favorable relative to earlier decades; the 2023 picture is more fragile: high food insecurity, malnutrition, street-children deprivation, and climate/disaster risks persisted. So the record is not “social failure,” but it is also not a clean story of durable, universally shared human development.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Long-run social gains existed, but vulnerability remained high**
- Fact: The World Bank’s poverty assessment says Bangladesh made major long-run gains in poverty reduction and access to basic services, but progress slowed after 2016 and large shares of the population remained vulnerable to falling back into poverty.
- Why it matters: This is the baseline for 6B.1, 6B.8, and 6B.10.
- Strong source: [World Bank poverty assessment executive summary](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099112625114013973)
- Locator: Summary findings in the executive summary.
- Period fit: Retrospective baseline, not 2023-only.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: This is a trajectory assessment, not a 2023 audit.

2. **Human development indicators were better than earlier decades**
- Fact: WHO says Bangladesh’s life expectancy at birth reached 73.1 years in 2021, up 7.74 years since 2000.
- Why it matters: This is strong context for 6B.10.
- Strong source: [WHO Bangladesh country profile](https://www.who.int/data/gho/data/countries/country-details/GHO/bangladesh?countryProfileId=7e4f2a3f-5b7a-4a7a-9a34-63a3365f00e3)
- Locator: Country overview and life-expectancy section.
- Period fit: Inherited trajectory, just before 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: It is not a pure 2023 result.

3. **Malnutrition remained widespread**
- Fact: UNICEF says 28 percent of Bangladeshi children under 5 were stunted, 10 percent were wasted, and early initiation of breastfeeding, exclusive breastfeeding, and adequate child feeding were still below ideal levels.
- Why it matters: This is core evidence for 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, and 6B.10.
- Strong source: [UNICEF Bangladesh nutrition page](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/nutrition)
- Locator: Lines 45-53.
- Period fit: Current context with direct relevance to 2023 conditions.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: UNICEF page is a program overview, not a 2023 survey release.

4. **Vulnerable groups faced sharper nutrition risks**
- Fact: UNICEF says children in urban slums, tea gardens, Rohingya camps, the poorest families, and households with less-educated mothers were more likely to suffer from stunting and wasting.
- Why it matters: This is direct evidence for 6B.3 and 6B.8.
- Strong source: [UNICEF Bangladesh nutrition page](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/nutrition)
- Locator: Lines 47-48.
- Period fit: Current context, relevant to 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for vulnerability mapping.
- Cautions: Not a 2023 incident report.

5. **Street children faced severe deprivation**
- Fact: UNICEF and the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics reported that children living on the street in Bangladesh faced deprivation, extreme poverty, malnutrition, disease, illiteracy, and violence.
- Why it matters: This is a concrete social-protection failure.
- Strong source: [UNICEF Bangladesh press release, 10 Apr. 2023](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/press-releases/unicef-new-report-details-shocking-deprivation-children-living-street)
- Locator: Opening paragraphs.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The report is about a specific vulnerable population, not the whole country.

6. **Food insecurity was large and persistent**
- Fact: WFP’s Bangladesh country page says 16 million people were facing acute food insecurity and 1.6 million young children were acutely malnourished, with inflation, weather shocks, and economic pressure among the drivers.
- Why it matters: This is one of the strongest 2023 welfare findings.
- Strong source: [WFP Bangladesh country page](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 253-265.
- Period fit: Current overview reflecting the 2023-2024 environment.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Some figures are current and retrospective, so they should be used carefully for pure 2023 attribution.

7. **The government and WFP worked together on social protection and school feeding**
- Fact: WFP says it helps Bangladesh reach vulnerable people through cash assistance, nutrition support, and skills training, and works with the government to improve major schemes such as the Vulnerable Women Benefit Programme and Mother and Child Benefit Programme.
- Why it matters: This is good evidence for 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, and 6B.9.
- Strong source: [WFP Bangladesh country page](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 283-287.
- Period fit: Current, but operationally relevant to 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: WFP is describing its own program role, not auditing overall government performance.

8. **School feeding was being relaunched as a national program**
- Fact: WFP says the government was launching a new national school feeding programme aimed at 3 million children over three years, after a nearly three-year pause.
- Why it matters: This is a concrete positive policy choice for 6B.2, 6B.6, and 6B.9.
- Strong source: [WFP Bangladesh country page](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 267-269 and 289-293.
- Period fit: 2023 transition into implementation.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Launch intent does not guarantee execution quality.

9. **Disaster risk remained a structural threat to well-being**
- Fact: WFP says Bangladesh is extremely disaster-prone and that it works with the government on anticipatory action, flood and cyclone response, and climate resilience.
- Why it matters: This is central to 6B.6.
- Strong source: [WFP Bangladesh country page](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 264-266 and 296-300.
- Period fit: Current context relevant to 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: This does not by itself prove specific 2023 disaster response quality.

10. **The state and UN system emphasized evidence-based nutrition policy**
- Fact: UNICEF says it supported the government in developing and implementing evidence-based nutrition policies and in monitoring financing for nutrition.
- Why it matters: This is evidence for 6B.5 and 6B.9.
- Strong source: [UNICEF Bangladesh nutrition page](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/nutrition)
- Locator: Lines 58-60.
- Period fit: Current program context.
- Attribution: Strong for policy process, weaker for outcome.
- Cautions: This is programmatic collaboration, not independent verification of success.

11. **Bangladesh’s health system still underinvested by global standards**
- Fact: WHO data show current health expenditure was only 2.36 percent of GDP in 2021, indicating limited fiscal depth for social protection and health delivery.
- Why it matters: This is useful for 6B.2 and 6B.9.
- Strong source: [WHO Bangladesh country profile](https://www.who.int/data/gho/data/countries/country-details/GHO/bangladesh?countryProfileId=7e4f2a3f-5b7a-4a7a-9a34-63a3365f00e3)
- Locator: Population and expenditure summary.
- Period fit: Pre-2023 baseline.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Health spending is a partial proxy for welfare effort.

12. **The UN results reporting confirms social-development activity continued in 2023**
- Fact: The UN country-results reporting exists as a 2023 accountability product for Bangladesh, showing ongoing social-development programming across the UN system.
- Why it matters: This is a documentation anchor for deeper work.
- Strong source: [United Nations Country Results Report Bangladesh 2023](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/273222-united-nations-country-results-report-bangladesh-2023)
- Locator: Report landing page and metadata.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: The landing page is not substantive evidence by itself.

**Lens Disposition**

- **6B.1 Welfare as a governing purpose:** Partial support. There was real social spending and program activity, but the evidence also shows strong political and crisis pressures.
- **6B.10 Lasting life chances:** Mixed. Long-run life chances improved, but 2023 did not clearly add a decisive new step forward.
- **6B.2 Access to essential services:** Partial. There were major programs and broad service gains, but nutrition, food security, and access quality remained uneven.
- **6B.3 Priority for vulnerable groups:** Partial to adverse. Vulnerable groups were targeted by programs, but they still faced elevated deprivation.
- **6B.4 Professional service delivery:** Partial. Program partnerships existed, but I did not build a personnel-quality file deep enough to call this strong.
- **6B.5 Measurement and correction:** Partial. There is evidence of evidence-based monitoring and collaboration, but not a full correction audit.
- **6B.6 Protection during crises:** Partial. Disaster and food-shock response systems existed, but need deeper event-level checking.
- **6B.7 No political allocation of welfare:** Open/uncertain. I did not yet assemble enough evidence on partisan allocation or punishment.
- **6B.8 Dignity and equal opportunity:** Mixed. There were continuing gains in access, but significant exclusion remained for poor, urban, slum, and marginalized children.
- **6B.9 Durable social institutions:** Partial. WFP, UNICEF, and the government were maintaining institutional systems, but durability and execution quality remain to be proven.

- [Bangladesh Development Update 2024 press release](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/feature/2024/04/05/strong-financial-sector-fiscal-and-monetary-policy-reforms-will-be-critical-to-sustain-bangladesh-s-growth-momentum)

**Uninspected leads**
- Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics income, poverty, and household survey pages.
- Bangladesh Bank annual report and monthly economic trends pages for welfare-adjacent inflation and household pressure.
- UNICEF 2023 MICS or other child-welfare releases if needed for better 2023 precision.
- UNFPA Bangladesh 2023 annual report for women’s health and protection.
- CAG audit pages for social-program execution and leakage.
- BBS latest publications for education, health, and poverty data.

**Research Gaps**
- I did not yet extract the underlying Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics tables for poverty, child nutrition, or service access.
- I did not build a full program-execution audit trail for social protection or health.
- I did not isolate regional inequality or urban-rural distribution in enough detail.
- I did not separately verify whether welfare allocation was politically neutral.
- I did not deeply inspect the UN 2023 country-results report contents, only its landing page.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

**Orientation**

Hasina’s 2023 integrity record is dominated by a pattern of denial, non-transparency, and impunity rather than by a clean corruption scandal with a single dispositive finding. The strongest evidence is indirect but convergent: she publicly dismissed corruption claims in parliament, her government’s information environment was highly restrictive, independent accountability mechanisms were weak or obstructed, and a close family member’s WHO elevation in late 2023 immediately raised credible nepotism concerns. I did not find a 2023 court judgment or audit that conclusively proved personal enrichment by Hasina herself. The better-supported conclusion is narrower: the ruler consistently protected power, reputation, and allied networks by rejecting allegations, praising loyalist claims, and operating in a system where independent scrutiny was limited.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Hasina publicly denied corruption allegations and demanded proof**
- Fact: In January 2023, Hasina told parliament that allegations of corruption in megaprojects were “completely baseless” and challenged critics to produce concrete figures.
- Why it matters: This is a direct truthfulness/denial record, and it shows how she handled adverse claims when truthfulness would have been politically costly.
- Strong source: [Prothom Alo / BSS report](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/parliament/bo14r310hf)
- Locator: Lines 83-86.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: It is a transcripted media report of a parliamentary answer, not a full verbatim Hansard.

2. **Hasina asserted election legitimacy and loyalty, minimizing opposition concerns**
- Fact: In the same parliamentary exchange, Hasina claimed people vote peacefully only when Awami League is in power and said the party “keeps the promises whatever it makes.”
- Why it matters: This is useful for assessing her public claims about performance and trustworthiness.
- Strong source: [Prothom Alo / BSS report](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/parliament/bo14r310hf)
- Locator: Lines 54-56 and 58-64.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Self-serving political rhetoric; should be treated as a statement, not a verified fact.

3. **The government and ruling-party environment severely constrained independent correction of falsehoods**
- Fact: Human Rights Watch says journalists, victims, and rights defenders faced attacks, self-censorship, legal harassment, and intimidation in 2023, which made correction of false claims and exposure of wrongdoing difficult.
- Why it matters: This is the key context for why personal honesty could not be checked well.
- Strong source: [HRW World Report 2024: Bangladesh](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- Locator: Lines 69-77 and 62-66.
- Period fit: Directly 2023, with retrospective framing in 2024.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Broad rights synthesis, not a single case file.

4. **The Cyber Security Act preserved a coercive legal structure hostile to scrutiny**
- Fact: The Cyber Security Act, 2023 retained broad state powers over search, seizure, and arrest; HRW and UN experts said it preserved abusive elements from the Digital Security Act.
- Why it matters: This is central to obstruction, concealment, and information control.
- Strong sources:
  - [Cyber Security Act, 2023](https://bdlaws.minlaw.gov.bd/act-print-1457/section-print-52866.html)
  - [HRW World Report 2024: Bangladesh](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
- Locator:
  - Section 42 in the law.
  - HRW lines 59, 75-77.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The statute alone does not prove abusive use, but the enforcement record does.

5. **UN special procedures recorded that Bangladesh’s measures disproportionately restricted political rights**
- Fact: OHCHR’s special-procedures summary for July 2024 said measures taken under recent amendments to the Anti-Terrorism Act and International Crimes Tribunal law imposed unnecessary and disproportionate restrictions on association, assembly, expression, participation, and fair trial.
- Why it matters: This is a later but relevant accountability signal on obstruction and concealment of political power.
- Strong source: [OHCHR summary print 30514](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/SummaryPrint?id=30514)
- Locator: Lines 3-8.
- Period fit: Later-retrospective, but directly tied to the Hasina era and her party.
- Attribution: Strong for the existence of the allegation and the UN’s framing.
- Cautions: It is a 2024 communication, so it cannot be treated as pure 2023 evidence.

6. **The World Bank transparency/accountability project shows an institutional need, not a success story**
- Fact: Bangladesh had an active World Bank project on strengthening transparency and accountability, which itself indicates recognized weaknesses in public-integrity systems.
- Why it matters: This is useful for 7B.6 and 7B.7 as background on accountability capacity.
- Strong source: [World Bank project document](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099052325123061621)
- Locator: Project page and metadata.
- Period fit: Inherited/ongoing context.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: The project page is evidence of need and institutional design, not of Hasina’s personal conduct.

7. **Hasina’s close family benefited from politically connected international access**
- Fact: In November 2023, her daughter Saima Wazed was nominated by WHO member states as regional director for South-East Asia, a result widely reported as politically sensitive because she was the prime minister’s daughter.
- Why it matters: This is the clearest 7B.5 and 7B.9 evidence of family-network advantage around elite office.
- Strong sources:
  - [Prothom Alo coverage](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/qk6g642rim)
  - [BSS coverage](https://www.bssnews.net/news-flash/155951)
  - [Indian Express archive report](https://indianexpress.com/article/world/bangladesh-saima-wazed-regional-director-who-south-east-asia-9008957/)
- Locator: November 1, 2023 reporting on the election/nomination.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: Election to an international post is not itself proof of wrongdoing, but the family connection is material and was widely noted.

8. **The WHO appointment became a later corruption controversy**
- Fact: By 2025, Bangladesh’s Anti-Corruption Commission accused Saima Wazed of fraud, forgery, and abuse of power in relation to her WHO campaign; later reporting says the appointment had been shadowed by allegations that Hasina used her influence.
- Why it matters: This is retrospective but relevant to whether the family network was merely prestigious or arguably self-dealing.
- Strong sources:
  - [Health Policy Watch](https://healthpolicy-watch.news/who-regional-director-saima-wazed-accused-of-fraud-and-forgery-in-her-election-campaign/)
  - [BSS report on leave amid corruption allegations](https://www.bssnews.net/news/291424)
- Locator: Opening paragraphs and charge summary.
- Period fit: Later-retrospective.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: This concerns Saima Wazed, not a formal finding against Sheikh Hasina herself; use as corroborative context only.

9. **Ruling-party rhetoric normalized refusal to acknowledge opposition legitimacy**
- Fact: Hasina repeatedly framed Awami League rule as synonymous with service to the people and denied the legitimacy of opposition criticism.
- Why it matters: This is relevant to ethical example and trust because it normalizes a winner-takes-all political culture.
- Strong source: [Prothom Alo / BSS report](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/parliament/bo14r310hf)
- Locator: Lines 39-47 and 54-66.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Rhetoric alone does not prove factual dishonesty, but it is part of the public record.

10. **Independent reporting treated Bangladesh as a restricted environment for scrutiny**
- Fact: HRW described a climate of attacks on journalists, self-censorship, and legal pressure, while RSF and CPJ also documented press-freedom abuse.
- Why it matters: This weakens confidence in any public claim of personal honesty or clean conduct.
- Strong sources:
  - [HRW World Report 2024: Bangladesh](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/bangladesh)
  - [RSF Bangladesh 2023](https://rsf.org/en/analyse_regionale/854)
  - [CPJ report on journalists attacked while covering rallies](https://cpj.org/2023/11/at-least-27-bangladeshi-journalists-attacked-harassed-while-covering-political-rallies)
- Locator: HRW lines 69-77; RSF country profile; CPJ headline and summary.
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong for the environment.
- Cautions: These are not bribery findings; they are accountability-environment findings.

**Lens Disposition**

- **7B.1 Truthfulness:** Partial/adverse. Hasina made direct self-serving denials and strong claims, but the surrounding environment did not support robust verification.
- **7B.10 Ethical example and public trust:** Adverse. Her conduct and the broader regime normalized denial, impunity, and loyalty-based legitimacy.
- **7B.2 Correcting falsehoods and errors:** Adverse/uncertain. I found no credible evidence of good-faith correction; the record tilts toward denial and retaliation.
- **7B.3 Conflicts of interest:** Partial. No formal conflict-of-interest regime evidence was recovered here, but the family-network pattern is concerning.
- **7B.4 Personal profit from office:** Unproven for Hasina personally in the 2023 record. I found concern, not a dispositive finding.
- **7B.5 Nepotism and loyalist appointments:** Adverse. The Saima Wazed WHO nomination is the clearest family-network advantage.
- **7B.6 Independent investigation:** Adverse. Independent scrutiny was weak and frequently obstructed.
- **7B.7 Obstruction and concealment:** Adverse. The legal-information environment and speech controls strongly favored concealment.
- **7B.8 Promises and good faith:** Mixed to adverse. Hasina publicly claimed loyalty and promise-keeping, but the record does not show a transparent standard of correction when challenged.
- **7B.9 Favoritism and clientelism:** Partial/adverse. Strongest evidence is family-connected access and a broader patronage-friendly political ecosystem.

- Tax, procurement, or foundation records tied to Hasina family interests.
- Any 2023 audit or court decision on megaproject corruption claims.
- Any primary transcript of the parliamentary session beyond the media report.

- I did not find a 2023 legal finding that Sheikh Hasina personally profited from office.
- I did not recover direct asset declarations or tax records.
- I did not fully inspect the WHO nomination proceedings, only media reporting on them.
- I did not locate a formal parliamentary ethics or conflict-of-interest regime with enforcement outcomes.
- I did not get a clean primary transcript of the January 2023 parliamentary exchange.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

**Orientation**

Sheikh Hasina’s 2023 effectiveness record is mixed. The strongest evidence supports a narrow, defensible claim: her government could still mobilize large state systems, pass budgets, sustain macro-policy response, and keep major development and social programs moving under external stress. But the same record also shows clear limits: inflation and FX pressure persisted, opposition-heavy politics distorted implementation, and several programs were continuation/repair efforts rather than decisive new breakthroughs. In other words, Bangladesh under Hasina in 2023 looked administratively capable in some domains, but not consistently adaptive, transparent, or resilient enough to count as a clean effectiveness success.

The biggest 2023 shocks were macroeconomic strain, pre-election political conflict, and the need to keep social and development systems operating under tighter fiscal and external conditions. The clearest positive pattern is program continuity: budgets were passed, IMF-supported correction was underway, social and welfare programs were maintained, and the state kept large-scale service and infrastructure systems functioning. The clearest negative pattern is that the government was often reactive rather than strategically corrective.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Hasina’s declared program was broad, developmental, and “Smart Bangladesh” oriented**
- Fact: She repeatedly framed the governing program around development, welfare, infrastructure, and national transformation, rather than a narrow crisis-only agenda.
- Why it matters: This is the starting point for 8B.1 and 8B.2.
- Strong sources:
  - [Awami League manifesto / speech materials](https://albd.org/articles/news/41214/Awami-League-unveils-election-manifesto-prioritising-11-key-areas)
  - [Prothom Alo transcript-style reporting on the election address](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/b9lhp1bv6t)
  - [UN General Debate address](https://webtv.un.org/en/asset/k1f/k1fod9fp2f)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Party and campaign language overstates control and success.

2. **The 2023-24 budget was enacted and publicly framed as transformational**
- Fact: The FY24 budget passed and was presented as a tool to accelerate Bangladesh’s transition to developed-country status.
- Why it matters: This shows conversion of program into formal fiscal machinery.
- Strong sources:
  - [BSS budget report](https://www.bssnews.net/news/132401)
  - [Budget archive](https://mof.gov.bd/site/page/591b7120-945c-4b88-9bd5-3447963f6249)
  - [Cabinet budget archive](https://cabinet.gov.bd/pages/static-pages/694032d635ce18e1c056333e)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Passing a budget is not the same as executing it well.

3. **The state had working macro-policy machinery, but it was under stress**
- Fact: IMF and World Bank material show Bangladesh had a functioning macro-policy apparatus that could negotiate with the IMF, adjust exchange-rate policy, and respond to inflation and reserve stress.
- Why it matters: This is core evidence for 8B.3, 8B.5, and 8B.9.
- Strong sources:
  - [IMF PR 23/440](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/12/12/pr23440-bangladesh-imf-exec-board-concludes-2023-art-iv-consult-completes-1st-rev-ecf-eef-rsf)
  - [IMF country statement](https://www.imf.org/en/publications/cr/issues/2023/12/13/bangladesh-2023-article-iv-consultation-first-reviews-under-the-extended-credit-facility-542460)
  - [World Bank Bangladesh Development Update](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099544204102498672)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: These are repair-and-stabilization signals, not proof of durable success.

4. **The government and central bank made a specific corrective FX move**
- Fact: Bangladesh Bank required export receipts to be converted at the market rate prevailing when they should have been realized, showing a concrete corrective response to FX pressure.
- Why it matters: This is one of the clearest 8B.8 evidence items.
- Strong source: [IMF staff report](https://www.elibrary.imf.org/view/journals/002/2023/410/article-A002-en.xml)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: Corrective policy does not guarantee success.

5. **Inflation, reserves, and growth pressures limited performance**
- Fact: FY23 growth slowed and inflation hit 9.9 percent in August 2023, while reserves and the taka remained under pressure.
- Why it matters: This is the main adverse context for 8B.7 and 8B.9.
- Strong source: [IMF PR 23/440](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/12/12/pr23440-bangladesh-imf-exec-board-concludes-2023-art-iv-consult-completes-1st-rev-ecf-eef-rsf)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Global shocks mattered, so not all underperformance is attributable to the ruler.

6. **The state could still mobilize major social programs**
- Fact: WFP and UNICEF reporting show continued operation of school feeding, nutrition, cash support, and vulnerable-group programs in 2023.
- Why it matters: This is evidence of translation from plans to practice in the welfare domain.
- Strong sources:
  - [WFP Bangladesh](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
  - [UNICEF nutrition page](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/nutrition)
  - [UNICEF street-children release](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/press-releases/unicef-new-report-details-shocking-deprivation-children-living-street)
- Period fit: Directly 2023 or immediately adjacent.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: These are program-implementation signals, not proof that outcomes matched ambition.

7. **The government and partners strengthened statistics and monitoring**
- Fact: WFP and Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics signed a cooperation agreement in late 2023 to strengthen official statistics and food-security monitoring.
- Why it matters: This is good evidence for 8B.5, 8B.6, and 8B.8.
- Strong source: [WFP statistics agreement](https://www.wfp.org/news/wfp-and-bangladesh-sign-agreement-strengthen-national-statistics-sdg-monitoring)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: The agreement shows measurement effort, not success by itself.

8. **The UN system continued to document and support Bangladesh’s social-development machinery**
- Fact: UN country-results reporting and UN-backed strategy work show a continuing official framework for development coordination in 2023.
- Why it matters: This supports claims about sustained institutional activity.
- Strong sources:
  - [UN Country Results Report Bangladesh 2023](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/273222-united-nations-country-results-report-bangladesh-2023)
  - [UN Country Results Report Bangladesh 2024](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/294308-united-nations-country-results-report-bangladesh-2024)
  - [SDGs Communications Strategy launch](https://bangladesh.un.org/en/272587-bangladesh-presents-first-national-sdgs-communications-strategy-and-action-plan-dhaka)
- Period fit: Directly 2023 or immediately after.
- Attribution: Moderate to strong.
- Cautions: The landing pages are thin; the substantive reports need deeper extraction later.

9. **Welfare and service delivery reached many people, but quality and vulnerability remained uneven**
- Fact: Long-run service gains existed, yet the population remained vulnerable to malnutrition, street-child deprivation, and food insecurity.
- Why it matters: This is the central mixed result for 8B.7.
- Strong sources:
  - [World Bank poverty assessment](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099112625114013973)
  - [UNICEF nutrition](https://www.unicef.org/bangladesh/en/nutrition)
  - [WFP Bangladesh](https://www.wfp.org/countries/bangladesh)
- Period fit: Long-run baseline plus 2023 context.
- Attribution: Moderate.
- Cautions: Strong development performance in some areas does not erase uneven quality and access.

10. **The state had capacity for major projects and digital-government work, but implementation still lagged**
- Fact: World Bank project documentation shows ongoing digital-government and accountability work, indicating the administration could carry out large reform projects but still needed external support.
- Why it matters: This is relevant to 8B.2, 8B.4, and 8B.6.
- Strong sources:
  - [Enhancing Digital Government & Economy ISR](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099071024204530586)
  - [Transparency and Accountability project](https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099052325123061621)
- Period fit: Directly 2023 or current implementation context.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Project existence is not the same as successful institutionalization.

11. **Hasina’s own messaging emphasized capability, continuity, and facing problems**
- Fact: Her public speeches and interview-style remarks repeatedly stressed that problems had to be faced, not denied, and that the government could continue development despite pressure.
- Why it matters: This is relevant to 8B.1 and 8B.9.
- Strong sources:
  - [Prothom Alo speech/report items](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/government/37k1kbd8gb)
  - [UN General Debate address](https://webtv.un.org/en/asset/k1f/k1fod9fp2f)
  - [General election address item](https://en.prothomalo.com/bangladesh/b9lhp1bv6t)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Rhetoric should not be confused with verified delivery.

12. **The regime’s effectiveness was constrained by political conflict and low trust**
- Fact: Political violence, opposition boycott threats, and weak institutional checks limited the government’s ability to convert programs into broadly legitimate outcomes.
- Why it matters: This is the main explanation for why the effectiveness record is mixed rather than strong.
- Strong sources:
  - [Freedom House 2024](https://freedomhouse.org/country/bangladesh/freedom-world/2024)
  - [Reuters via ThePrint](https://theprint.in/world/bangladeshs-main-opposition-to-boycott-vote-if-hasina-stays-put/1827169/)
  - [ACLED election violence reports](https://acleddata.com/report/bangladesh-heads-toward-landmark-election-amid-rising-political-violence)
- Period fit: Directly 2023.
- Attribution: Strong.
- Cautions: Effectiveness and legitimacy are related but not identical.

**Lens Disposition**

- **8B.1 Clear governing program:** Supported. The program was clearly developmental, pro-welfare, and “Smart Bangladesh” oriented.
- **8B.10 Durable goal achievement:** Mixed. Some durable institutional and service gains existed, but 2023 also showed fragility and incomplete conversion into stable outcomes.
- **8B.2 Turning goals into machinery:** Partial to strong. Budgets, programs, and monitoring systems existed and were active.
- **8B.3 Mobilizing the ruling system:** Partial. The state clearly mobilized, but political conflict and macro stress limited reach.
- **8B.4 Capable implementers:** Partial. There is evidence of functioning technocratic and implementing systems, but I did not recover enough personnel-specific evidence to rate this strongly.
- **8B.5 Coordination and control:** Partial. Coordination was real, especially in macro and social programs, but not cleanly efficient.
- **8B.6 From plans to practice:** Partial to strong. Several plans became real action, especially budgets, IMF-linked correction, social programs, and monitoring arrangements.
- **8B.7 Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed. Social and development gains continued, but macro stress and weak trust limited progress.
- **8B.8 Learning and correction:** Partial. The FX policy adjustment and IMF engagement show some learning, but mostly reactive.
- **8B.9 Managing crises and resistance:** Partial. The government kept core programs running amid crises, but not without significant strain.

**Uninspected leads**
- Full text of the Awami League manifesto and Hasina’s 2023 manifesto speech.
- Bangladesh Ministry of Finance 2023-24 budget speech PDF.
- Bangladesh Bank annual report and monetary-policy archive details.
- The full 2023 and 2024 UN country-results reports.
- The ILO Bangladesh country programme and evaluation documents.
- Detailed Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics publications on services, labor, and household conditions.

**Research Gaps**
- I did not build a detailed personnel file on technocrats, secretaries, or program implementers.
- I did not fully extract budget execution tables, only formal budget and policy evidence.
- I did not separate announced goals from execution in every sector.
- I did not inspect the full manifesto speech text, which would strengthen 8B.1 and 8B.2.
- I did not get fine-grained milestone-by-ministry implementation evidence; most of this pass is high-level and macro/programmatic.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

I resumed the 1B nuclear/catastrophic-risk file and expanded the source pool across IAEA, BAERA, UNODA, OPCW, Rosatom-linked reporting, Reuters-fed coverage, and Bangladesh official statements. The record still saturates early: Bangladesh had no nuclear weapons, did have a civilian nuclear buildout at Rooppur, and maintained a formal safeguards/regulatory framework; I found no evidence of reckless nuclear threats, proliferation, or use of nuclear capability for coercion. The main remaining gap is independent verification of 2023 safety/command practice, especially around Rooppur’s operational controls.

I could not write `research-ledger-manifest.json` in this session because the workspace is read-only. I’m therefore providing a manifest-style delta in the memo below.

**1B Orientation**
- Bangladesh’s 2023 catastrophic-risk posture was civilian and treaty-bound, not weaponized.
- The material 2023 choices were to keep Rooppur moving, manage Russian sanctions/payment friction, and publicly reaffirm peaceful-use commitments.
- Formal safeguards and regulatory institutions existed, but independent evidence on their practical effectiveness in 2023 is thin.
- The best-supported conclusion is “civilian nuclear expansion under formal nonproliferation constraints,” not “demonstrated safe execution.”

**Evidence Index**
1. Bangladesh was an IAEA member with an in-force NPT safeguards agreement and Additional Protocol.
2. Bangladesh was also a party to key physical-protection and nuclear-emergency conventions.
3. The 2025 IAEA CNPP profile shows Rooppur as a two-reactor civilian nuclear project under construction, with long-term planning for commercial operation.
4. In April 2023 Bangladesh switched Rooppur loan settlement to yuan because Russia-related sanctions blocked ordinary dollar payment channels.
5. In September-October 2023 the first uranium fuel shipment arrived and was formally handed over, with heavy security for transport.
6. Bangladesh’s UN First Committee statement in October 2023 reaffirmed peaceful nuclear use and disarmament principles.
7. BAERA’s 2023 administrative reporting shows an active regulator with formal annual-plan reporting, but not independent proof of effective oversight.
8. OPCW and UN treaty records help confirm Bangladesh’s broader disarmament/implementation posture for WMD-related regimes, but they do not show a nuclear-weapons risk.

**Lens Disposition**
- 1B.1 Reducing existential risk: partial support, mainly through peaceful-use commitments and safeguards.
- 1B.10 Lasting risk posture: partial. Bangladesh ended 2023 still non-weaponized and treaty-bound, but practical safety assurance remains weakly evidenced.
- 1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric: supported. Official rhetoric consistently emphasized peaceful use and nuclear disarmament.
- 1B.3 Safe nuclear control: partial. Formal regulator and transport/security arrangements exist; independent effectiveness evidence remains thin.
- 1B.4 Arms control and inspections: supported on baseline treaty status and compliance architecture.
- 1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression: no evidence found.
- 1B.6 Preventing proliferation: supported at the formal level, with no contrary evidence found in the 2023 record.
- 1B.7 Independent risk expertise: partial. BAERA/IAEA-facing structures exist, but independence from executive power is not established.
- 1B.8 Crisis de-escalation: no direct crisis case found in 2023.
- 1B.9 Other catastrophic risks: weak/indirect only. I did not find a 2023 AI/biotech catastrophic-risk policy record worth treating as evidence.

- The chapter is likely saturated for “no nuclear weapons / civilian buildout” but not for “how safe was the execution.”
